(src-averill-1-0-ch16)=

# Chapter 16 Aqueous Acid--Base Equilibriums

Many vital chemical and physical processes take place exclusively in aqueous solution, including the complex biochemical reactions that occur in living organisms and the reactions that rust and corrode steel objects, such as bridges, ships, and automobiles. Among the most important reactions in aqueous solution are those that can be categorized as acid--base, precipitation, and complexation reactions. So far, our discussions of these reactions have been largely qualitative. In this chapter and [Chapter 17 \"Solubility and Complexation Equilibriums\"](/chapters/ch-17-solubility-and-complexation-eq.md#src-averill-1-0-ch17), however, we take a more quantitative approach to understanding such reactions, using the concept of chemical equilibrium that we developed in [Chapter 15 \"Chemical Equilibrium\"](/chapters/ch-15-chemical-equilibrium.md#src-averill-1-0-ch15) for simple gas-phase reactions. We will begin by revisiting acid--base reactions in a qualitative fashion and then develop quantitative methods to describe acid--base equilibriums. In [Chapter 17 \"Solubility and Complexation Equilibriums\"](/chapters/ch-17-solubility-and-complexation-eq.md#src-averill-1-0-ch17), we will use the same approach to describe the equilibriums involved in the dissolution of sparingly soluble solids and the formation of metal complexes.

![Indicators are used to monitor changes in pH. The pH of a solution can be monitored using an acid--base indicator, a substance that undergoes a color change within a specific pH range that is characteristic of that indicator. The color changes for seven commonly used indicators over a pH range of 1--10 are shown here.](/images/section_20/5b87c7da3887d432378a9972b230e850.jpg)

**Indicators are used to monitor changes in pH.** The pH of a solution can be monitored using an acid--base indicator, a substance that undergoes a color change within a specific pH range that is characteristic of that indicator. The color changes for seven commonly used indicators over a pH range of 1--10 are shown here.

In [Chapter 4 \"Reactions in Aqueous Solution\"](/chapters/ch-04-reactions-in-aqueous-solution.md#src-averill-1-0-ch04), we described how acid rain can adversely affect the survival of marine life and plant growth. Many significant phenomena, such as acid rain, can be understood only in terms of the acid--base behavior of chemical species. As we expand our discussion of acid--base behavior in this chapter, you will learn why lemon slices are served with fish, why the strengths of acids and bases can vary over many orders of magnitude, and why rhubarb leaves are toxic to humans. You will also understand how the pH of your blood is kept constant, even though you produce large amounts of acid when you exercise.

(src-averill-1-0-ch16-s01)=

## 16.1 The Autoionization of Water

````````````{note} Learning Objectives

1.  To understand the autoionization reaction of liquid water.
2.  To know the relationship among pH, pOH, and p*K*<sub>w</sub>.

````````````

As you learned in [Chapter 4 \"Reactions in Aqueous Solution\"](/chapters/ch-04-reactions-in-aqueous-solution.md#src-averill-1-0-ch04) and [Chapter 8 \"Ionic versus Covalent Bonding\"](/chapters/ch-08-ionic-versus-covalent-bonding.md#src-averill-1-0-ch08), acids and bases can be defined in several different ways ([Table 16.1 \"Definitions of Acids and Bases\"](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s01-t01)). Recall that the Arrhenius definition of an acid is a substance that dissociates in water to produce H<sup>+</sup> ions (protons), and an Arrhenius base is a substance that dissociates in water to produce OH<sup>−</sup> (hydroxide) ions. According to this view, an acid--base reaction involves the reaction of a proton with a hydroxide ion to form water. Although Brønsted and Lowry defined an acid similarly to Arrhenius by describing an acid as any substance that can *donate* a proton, the Brønsted--Lowry definition of a base is much more general than the Arrhenius definition. In Brønsted--Lowry terms, a base is any substance that can *accept* a proton, so a base is not limited to just a hydroxide ion. This means that for every Brønsted--Lowry acid, there exists a corresponding conjugate base with one fewer proton, as we demonstrated in [Chapter 4 \"Reactions in Aqueous Solution\"](/chapters/ch-04-reactions-in-aqueous-solution.md#src-averill-1-0-ch04). Consequently, all Brønsted--Lowry acid--base reactions actually involve *two* conjugate acid--base pairs and the transfer of a *proton* from one substance (the acid) to another (the base). In contrast, the Lewis definition of acids and bases, discussed in [Chapter 8 \"Ionic versus Covalent Bonding\"](/chapters/ch-08-ionic-versus-covalent-bonding.md#src-averill-1-0-ch08), focuses on accepting or donating *pairs of electrons* rather than protons. A Lewis base is an electron-pair donor, and a Lewis acid is an electron-pair acceptor.

(src-averill-1-0-ch16-s01-t01)=

Table 16.1 Definitions of Acids and Bases

````````{list-table}
:header-rows: 1

- -  
  - Acids
  - Bases
- - Arrhenius
  - H<sup>+</sup> donor
  - OH<sup>−</sup> donor
- - Brønsted--Lowry
  - H<sup>+</sup> donor
  - H<sup>+</sup> acceptor
- - Lewis
  - electron-pair acceptor
  - electron-pair donor

````````

Because this chapter deals with acid--base equilibriums in *aqueous solution*, our discussion will use primarily the Brønsted--Lowry definitions and nomenclature. Remember, however, that all three definitions are just different ways of looking at the same kind of reaction: a proton is an acid, and the hydroxide ion is a base---no matter which definition you use. In practice, chemists tend to use whichever definition is most helpful to make a particular point or understand a given system. If, for example, we refer to a base as having one or more lone pairs of electrons that can accept a proton, we are simply combining the Lewis and Brønsted--Lowry definitions to emphasize the characteristic properties of a base.

In [Chapter 4 \"Reactions in Aqueous Solution\"](/chapters/ch-04-reactions-in-aqueous-solution.md#src-averill-1-0-ch04), we also introduced the acid--base properties of water, its *autoionization reaction*, and the definition of pH. The purpose of this section is to review those concepts and describe them using the concepts of chemical equilibrium developed in [Chapter 15 \"Chemical Equilibrium\"](/chapters/ch-15-chemical-equilibrium.md#src-averill-1-0-ch15).

### Acid--Base Properties of Water

The structure of the water molecule, with its polar O--H bonds and two lone pairs of electrons on the oxygen atom, was described in [Chapter 4 \"Reactions in Aqueous Solution\"](/chapters/ch-04-reactions-in-aqueous-solution.md#src-averill-1-0-ch04) and [Chapter 8 \"Ionic versus Covalent Bonding\"](/chapters/ch-08-ionic-versus-covalent-bonding.md#src-averill-1-0-ch08), and the structure of liquid water was discussed in [Chapter 13 \"Solutions\"](/chapters/ch-13-solutions.md#src-averill-1-0-ch13). Recall that because of its highly polar structure, liquid water can act as either an acid (by donating a proton to a base) or a base (by using a lone pair of electrons to accept a proton). For example, when a strong acid such as HCl dissolves in water, it dissociates into chloride ions (Cl<sup>−</sup>) and protons (H<sup>+</sup>). As you learned in [Chapter 4 \"Reactions in Aqueous Solution\"](/chapters/ch-04-reactions-in-aqueous-solution.md#src-averill-1-0-ch04), the proton, in turn, reacts with a water molecule to form the *hydronium ion* (H<sub>3</sub>O<sup>+</sup>):

(src-averill-1-0-ch16-s01-s01-eq01)=

Equation 16.1

$$\left. \underset{\text{acid}}{\text{HCl(aq)~}} + \underset{\text{base}}{\text{H}_{2}\text{O(l)}}\rightarrow\underset{\text{acid}}{\text{H}_{3}\text{O}^{+}\text{(aq)~}} + \underset{\text{base}}{\text{Cl}^{-}\text{(aq)}} \right.$$

In this reaction, HCl is the acid, and water acts as a base by accepting an H<sup>+</sup> ion. The reaction in [Equation 16.1](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s01-s01-eq01) is often written in a simpler form by removing H<sub>2</sub>O from each side:

(src-averill-1-0-ch16-s01-s01-eq02)=

Equation 16.2

HCl(aq) → H<sup>+</sup>(aq) + Cl<sup>−</sup>(aq)

In [Equation 16.2](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s01-s01-eq02), the hydronium ion is represented by H<sup>+</sup>, although free H<sup>+</sup> ions do not exist in liquid water.

Water can also act as an acid, as shown in [Equation 16.3](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s01-s01-eq03). In this equilibrium reaction, H<sub>2</sub>O donates a proton to NH<sub>3</sub>, which acts as a base:

(src-averill-1-0-ch16-s01-s01-eq03)=

Equation 16.3

$$\left. \underset{\text{acid}}{\text{H}_{2}\text{O(l)~}}\text{~+~}\underset{\text{base}}{\text{NH}_{3}\text{(aq)}}\rightleftharpoons\underset{\text{acid}}{\text{NH}_{4}{}^{+}(\text{aq)~}} + \underset{\text{base}}{\text{OH}^{-}\text{(aq)}} \right.$$

Thus water is amphiprotic[^chem-1], meaning that it can behave as either an acid or a base, depending on the nature of the other reactant. Notice that [Equation 16.3](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s01-s01-eq03) is an equilibrium reaction as indicated by the double arrow.

### The Ion-Product Constant of Liquid Water

Because water is amphiprotic, one water molecule can react with another to form an OH<sup>−</sup> ion and an H<sub>3</sub>O<sup>+</sup> ion in an autoionization process:

(src-averill-1-0-ch16-s01-s02-eq01)=

Equation 16.4

$$\left. \text{2H}_{2}\text{O(l)}\rightleftharpoons\text{H}_{3}\text{O}^{+}\text{(aq)} + \text{OH}^{-}\text{(aq)} \right.$$

The equilibrium constant *K* for this reaction can be written as follows:

(src-averill-1-0-ch16-s01-s02-eq02)=

Equation 16.5

$$K = \frac{\lbrack\text{H}_{3}\text{O}^{+}\rbrack\lbrack\text{OH}^{-}\rbrack}{{\lbrack\text{H}_{2}\text{O}\rbrack}^{2}}$$

When pure liquid water is in equilibrium with hydronium and hydroxide ions at 25°C, the concentrations of the hydronium ion and the hydroxide ion are equal: \[H<sub>3</sub>O<sup>+</sup>\] = \[OH<sup>−</sup>\] = 1.003 × 10<sup>−7</sup> M. Thus the number of dissociated water molecules is very small indeed, approximately 2 ppb. We can calculate \[H<sub>2</sub>O\] at 25°C from the density of water at this temperature (0.997 g/mL):

Equation 16.6

$$\lbrack\text{H}_{2}\text{O}\rbrack = \frac{\text{mol}}{\text{L}} = \left( \frac{0.997\text{~g}}{\text{mL}} \right)\left( \frac{1\text{~mol}}{18.02\text{~g}} \right)\left( \frac{1000\text{~mL}}{\text{L}} \right) = 55.3\text{~M}$$

With so few water molecules dissociated, the equilibrium of the autoionization reaction ([Equation 16.4](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s01-s02-eq01)) lies far to the left. Consequently, \[H<sub>2</sub>O\] is essentially unchanged by the autoionization reaction and can be treated as a constant. Incorporating this constant into the equilibrium expression allows us to rearrange [Equation 16.5](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s01-s02-eq02) to define a new equilibrium constant, the ion-product constant of liquid water (*K*<sub>w</sub>)[^chem-2]:

(src-averill-1-0-ch16-s01-s02-eq04)=

Equation 16.7

$$\begin{aligned}
{K{\lbrack\text{H}_{2}\text{O}\rbrack}^{2}} & {= \lbrack\text{H}_{3}\text{O}^{+}\rbrack\lbrack\text{OH}^{-}\rbrack} \\
K_{w} & {= \lbrack\text{H}_{3}\text{O}^{+}\rbrack\lbrack\text{OH}^{-}\rbrack}
\end{aligned}$$

Substituting the values for \[H<sub>3</sub>O<sup>+</sup>\] and \[OH<sup>−</sup>\] at 25°C into this expression,

Equation 16.8

$$K_{\text{w}} = (1.003 \times 10^{- 7})(1.003 \times 10^{- 7}) = 1.006 \times 10^{- 14}$$

Thus, to three significant figures, *K*<sub>w</sub> = 1.01 × 10<sup>−14</sup> M. Like any other equilibrium constant, *K*<sub>w</sub> varies with temperature, ranging from 1.15 × 10<sup>−15</sup> at 0°C to 4.99 × 10<sup>−13</sup> at 100°C.

In pure water, the concentrations of the hydronium ion and the hydroxide ion are equal, and the solution is therefore neutral. If \[H<sub>3</sub>O<sup>+</sup>\] \> \[OH<sup>−</sup>\], however, the solution is acidic, whereas if \[H<sub>3</sub>O<sup>+</sup>\] \< \[OH<sup>−</sup>\], the solution is basic. For an aqueous solution, the H<sub>3</sub>O<sup>+</sup> concentration is a quantitative measure of acidity: the higher the H<sub>3</sub>O<sup>+</sup> concentration, the more acidic the solution. Conversely, the higher the OH<sup>−</sup> concentration, the more basic the solution. In most situations that you will encounter, the H<sub>3</sub>O<sup>+</sup> and OH<sup>−</sup> concentrations from the dissociation of water are so small (1.003 × 10<sup>−7</sup> M) that they can be ignored in calculating the H<sub>3</sub>O<sup>+</sup> or OH<sup>−</sup> concentrations of solutions of acids and bases, but this is not always the case.

### The Relationship among pH, pOH, and p*K*<sub>w</sub>

The *pH scale* is a concise way of describing the H<sub>3</sub>O<sup>+</sup> concentration and hence the acidity or basicity of a solution. Recall from [Chapter 4 \"Reactions in Aqueous Solution\"](/chapters/ch-04-reactions-in-aqueous-solution.md#src-averill-1-0-ch04) that pH and the H<sup>+</sup> (H<sub>3</sub>O<sup>+</sup>) concentration are related as follows:

(src-averill-1-0-ch16-s01-s03-eq01)=

Equation 16.9

$$\text{pH} = - \log_{10}\lbrack\text{H}^{+}\rbrack$$

(src-averill-1-0-ch16-s01-s03-eq02)=

Equation 16.10

$$\lbrack\text{H}^{+}\rbrack = 10^{- \text{pH}}$$

Because the scale is logarithmic, a pH difference of *1* between two solutions corresponds to a difference of a factor of *10* in their hydronium ion concentrations. (Refer to Essential Skills 3 in [Chapter 4 \"Reactions in Aqueous Solution\"](/chapters/ch-04-reactions-in-aqueous-solution.md#src-averill-1-0-ch04), [Section 4.10 \"Essential Skills 3\"](/chapters/ch-04-reactions-in-aqueous-solution.md#src-averill-1-0-ch04-s10), if you need to refresh your memory about how to use logarithms.) Recall also that the pH of a neutral solution is 7.00 (\[H<sub>3</sub>O<sup>+</sup>\] = 1.0 × 10<sup>−7</sup> M), whereas acidic solutions have pH \< 7.00 (corresponding to \[H<sub>3</sub>O<sup>+</sup>\] \> 1.0 × 10<sup>−7</sup>) and basic solutions have pH \> 7.00 (corresponding to \[H<sub>3</sub>O<sup>+</sup>\] \< 1.0 × 10<sup>−7</sup>).

Similar notation systems are used to describe many other chemical quantities that contain a large negative exponent. For example, chemists use an analogous *pOH scale* to describe the hydroxide ion concentration of a solution. The pOH and \[OH<sup>−</sup>\] are related as follows:

(src-averill-1-0-ch16-s01-s03-eq03)=

Equation 16.11

$$\text{pOH} = - \log_{10}\lbrack\text{OH}^{-}\rbrack$$

(src-averill-1-0-ch16-s01-s03-eq04)=

Equation 16.12

$$\lbrack\text{OH}^{-}\rbrack = 10^{- \text{pOH}}$$

The constant *K*<sub>w</sub> can also be expressed using this notation, where p*K*<sub>w</sub> = −log *K*<sub>w</sub>.

Because a neutral solution has \[OH<sup>−</sup>\] = 1.0 × 10<sup>−7</sup>, the pOH of a neutral solution is 7.00. Consequently, the sum of the pH and the pOH for a neutral solution at 25°C is 7.00 + 7.00 = 14.00. We can show that the sum of pH and pOH is equal to 14.00 for *any* aqueous solution at 25°C by taking the negative logarithm of both sides of [Equation 16.7](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s01-s02-eq04):

(src-averill-1-0-ch16-s01-s03-eq05)=

Equation 16.13

$$\begin{aligned}
{- \log K_{\text{w}}} & {= - \log(\lbrack\text{H}_{3}\text{O}^{+}\rbrack\lbrack\text{OH}^{-}\rbrack)} \\
{\text{p}K_{\text{w}}} & {= ( - \log\lbrack\text{H}_{3}\text{O}^{+}\rbrack) + ( - \log\lbrack\text{OH}^{-}\rbrack)} \\
{\text{p}K_{\text{w}}} & {= \text{pH} + \text{pOH}}
\end{aligned}$$

Thus at any temperature, pH + pOH = p*K*<sub>w</sub>, so at 25°C, where *K*<sub>w</sub> = 1.0 × 10<sup>−14</sup>, pH + pOH = 14.00. More generally, the pH of any neutral solution is half of the p*K*<sub>w</sub> at that temperature. The relationship among pH, pOH, and the acidity or basicity of a solution is summarized graphically in [Figure 16.1 \"The Inverse Relationship between the pH and pOH Scales\"](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s01-s03-f01) over the common pH range of 0 to 14. Notice the inverse relationship between the pH and pOH scales.

````````````{important} Note the Pattern

For any neutral solution, pH + pOH = 14.00 (at 25°C) and $\text{pH} = \frac{1}{2}\text{p}K_{\text{w}}\text{.}$

````````````

(src-averill-1-0-ch16-s01-s03-f01)=

Figure 16.1 The Inverse Relationship between the pH and pOH Scales

![Figure 16.1 The Inverse Relationship between the pH and pOH Scales](/images/section_20/4d72bb2e90588a73f5bba9314bdf1abb.jpg)

As pH decreases, \[H<sup>+</sup>\] and the acidity increase. As pOH increases, \[OH<sup>−</sup>\] and the basicity decrease. Common substances have pH values that range from extremely acidic to extremely basic.

### Example 1

The *K*<sub>w</sub> for water at 100°C is 4.99 × 10<sup>−13</sup>. Calculate p*K*<sub>w</sub> for water at this temperature and the pH and the pOH for a neutral aqueous solution at 100°C. Report pH and pOH values to two decimal places.

**Given:** *K* <sub>w</sub>

**Asked for:** p*K*<sub>w</sub>, pH, and pOH

**Strategy:**

**A** Calculate p*K*<sub>w</sub> by taking the negative logarithm of *K*<sub>w</sub>.

**B** For a neutral aqueous solution, \[H<sub>3</sub>O<sup>+</sup>\] = \[OH<sup>−</sup>\]. Use this relationship and [Equation 16.7](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s01-s02-eq04) to calculate \[H<sub>3</sub>O<sup>+</sup>\] and \[OH<sup>−</sup>\]. Then determine the pH and the pOH for the solution.

**Solution:**

**A** Because p*K*<sub>w</sub> is the negative logarithm of *K*<sub>w</sub>, we can write

p*K*<sub>w</sub> = −log *K*<sub>w</sub> = −log(4.99 × 10<sup>−13</sup>) = 12.302

The answer is reasonable: *K*<sub>w</sub> is between 10<sup>−13</sup> and 10<sup>−12</sup>, so p*K*<sub>w</sub> must be between 12 and 13.

**B** [Equation 16.7](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s01-s02-eq04) shows that *K*<sub>w</sub> = \[H<sub>3</sub>O<sup>+</sup>\]\[OH<sup>−</sup>\]. Because \[H<sub>3</sub>O<sup>+</sup>\] = \[OH<sup>−</sup>\] in a neutral solution, we can let *x* = \[H<sub>3</sub>O<sup>+</sup>\] = \[OH<sup>−</sup>\]:

$$\begin{aligned}
K_{\text{w}} & {= \lbrack\text{H}_{3}\text{O}^{+}\rbrack\lbrack\text{OH}^{-}\rbrack = (x)(x) = x^{2}} \\
x & {= \sqrt{K_{\text{w}}} = \sqrt{4.99 \times 10^{- 13}} = 7.06 \times 10^{- 7}\text{~M}}
\end{aligned}$$

Because *x* is equal to both \[H<sub>3</sub>O<sup>+</sup>\] and \[OH<sup>−</sup>\],

pH = pOH = −log(7.06 × 10<sup>−7</sup>) = 6.15 (to two decimal places)

We could obtain the same answer more easily (without using logarithms) by using the p*K*<sub>w</sub>. In this case, we know that p*K*<sub>w</sub> = 12.302, and from [Equation 16.13](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s01-s03-eq05), we know that p*K*<sub>w</sub> = pH + pOH. Because pH = pOH in a neutral solution, we can use [Equation 16.13](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s01-s03-eq05) directly, setting pH = pOH = *y*. Solving to two decimal places we obtain the following:

$$\begin{aligned}
{\text{p}K_{\text{w}}} & {= \text{pH} + \text{pOH} = y + y = 2y} \\
y & {= \frac{\text{p}K_{\text{w}}}{2} = \frac{12.302}{2} = 6.15 = \text{pH} = \text{pOH}}
\end{aligned}$$

Exercise

Humans maintain an internal temperature of about 37°C. At this temperature, *K*<sub>w</sub> = 3.55 × 10<sup>−14</sup>. Calculate p*K*<sub>w</sub> and the pH and the pOH of a neutral solution at 37°C. Report pH and pOH values to two decimal places.

**Answer:** p*K*<sub>w</sub> = 13.45 pH = pOH = 6.73

````````````{important} Summary

Water is **amphiprotic**: it can act as an acid by donating a proton to a base to form the hydroxide ion, or as a base by accepting a proton from an acid to form the hydronium ion (H<sub>3</sub>O<sup>+</sup>). The *autoionization* of liquid water produces OH<sup>−</sup> and H<sub>3</sub>O<sup>+</sup> ions. The equilibrium constant for this reaction is called the **ion-product constant of liquid water (***K*<sub>**w**</sub>**)** and is defined as *K*<sub>w</sub> = \[H<sub>3</sub>O<sup>+</sup>\]\[OH<sup>−</sup>\]. At 25°C, *K*<sub>w</sub> is 1.01 × 10<sup>−14</sup>; hence pH + pOH = p*K*<sub>w</sub> = 14.00.

````````````

````````````{important} Key Takeaway

- For any neutral solution, pH + pOH = 14.00 (at 25°C) and pH = 1/2 p*K*<sub>w</sub>.

````````````

````````````{important} Key Equations

**Ion-product constant of liquid water**

[Equation 16.7](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s01-s02-eq04): *K*<sub>w</sub> = \[H<sub>3</sub>O<sup>+</sup>\]\[OH<sup>−</sup>\]

**Definition of pH**

[Equation 16.9](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s01-s03-eq01): pH = −log<sub>10</sub>\[H<sup>+</sup>\]

[Equation 16.10](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s01-s03-eq02): \[H<sup>+</sup>\] = 10<sup>−pH</sup>

**Definition of pOH**

[Equation 16.11](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s01-s03-eq03): pOH = −log<sub>10</sub>\[OH<sup>+</sup>\]

[Equation 16.12](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s01-s03-eq04): \[OH<sup>−</sup>\] = 10<sup>−pOH</sup>

**Relationship among pH, pOH, and p** *K* **<sub>w</sub>**

[Equation 16.13](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s01-s03-eq05): p*K*<sub>w</sub>= pH + pOH

````````````

### Conceptual Problems

1.  What is the relationship between the value of the equilibrium constant for the autoionization of liquid water and the tabulated value of the ion-product constant of liquid water (*K*<sub>w</sub>)?

2.  The density of liquid water decreases as the temperature increases from 25°C to 50°C. Will this effect cause *K*<sub>w</sub> to increase or decrease? Why?

3.  Show that water is amphiprotic by writing balanced chemical equations for the reactions of water with HNO<sub>3</sub> and NH<sub>3</sub>. In which reaction does water act as the acid? In which does it act as the base?

4.  Write a chemical equation for each of the following.

    1.  Nitric acid is added to water.
    2.  Potassium hydroxide is added to water.
    3.  Calcium hydroxide is added to water.
    4.  Sulfuric acid is added to water.

5.  Show that *K* for the sum of the following reactions is equal to *K*<sub>w</sub>.

    1.  $\left. \text{HMnO}_{4}\text{(aq)}\rightleftharpoons\text{H}^{+}\text{(aq)} + \text{MnO}_{4}{}^{-}\text{(aq)} \right.$
    2.  $\left. \text{MnO}_{4}{}^{-}\text{(aq)} + \text{H}_{2}\text{O(l)}\rightleftharpoons\text{HMnO}_{4}\text{(aq)} + \text{OH}^{-}\text{(aq)} \right.$

### Answers

1.

### Numerical Problems

1.  The autoionization of sulfuric acid can be described by the following chemical equation:

    $$\begin{array}{l}

    {\quad\text{H}_{3}\text{SO}_{4}{}^{+}\text{(soln)} + \text{HSO}_{4}{}^{-}\text{(soln)}}
    \end{array}$$

    At 25°C, *K* = 3 × 10<sup>−4</sup>. Write an equilibrium constant expression for $K_{\text{H}_{2}\text{SO}_{4}}$ that is analogous to *K*<sub>w</sub>. The density of H<sub>2</sub>SO<sub>4</sub> is 1.8 g/cm<sup>3</sup> at 25°C. What is the concentration of H<sub>3</sub>SO<sub>4</sub><sup>+</sup>? What fraction of H<sub>2</sub>SO<sub>4</sub> is ionized?

## 16.2 A Qualitative Description of Acid--Base Equilibriums

````````````{note} Learning Objectives

1.  To understand the concept of conjugate acid--base pairs.
2.  To know the relationship between acid or base strength and the magnitude of *K*<sub>a</sub>, *K*<sub>b</sub>, p*K*<sub>a</sub>, and p*K*<sub>b</sub>.
3.  To understand the leveling effect.

````````````

We now turn our attention to acid--base reactions to see how the concepts of chemical equilibrium and equilibrium constants can deepen our understanding of this kind of chemical behavior. We begin with a qualitative description of acid--base equilibriums in terms of the Brønsted--Lowry model and then proceed to a quantitative description in [Section 16.4 \"Quantitative Aspects of Acid--Base Equilibriums\"](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s04).

### Conjugate Acid--Base Pairs

We discussed the concept of conjugate acid--base pairs in [Chapter 4 \"Reactions in Aqueous Solution\"](/chapters/ch-04-reactions-in-aqueous-solution.md#src-averill-1-0-ch04), using the reaction of ammonia, the base, with water, the acid, as an example. In aqueous solutions, acids and bases can be defined in terms of the transfer of a proton from an acid to a base. Thus for every acidic species in an aqueous solution, there exists a species derived from the acid by the loss of a proton. These two species that differ by only a proton constitute a conjugate acid--base pair[^chem-3]. For example, in the reaction of HCl with water ([Equation 16.1](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s01-s01-eq01)), HCl, the *parent acid*, donates a proton to a water molecule, the *parent base*, thereby forming Cl<sup>−</sup>. Thus HCl and Cl<sup>−</sup> constitute a conjugate acid--base pair. By convention, we always write a conjugate acid--base pair as the acid followed by its conjugate base. In the reverse reaction, the Cl<sup>−</sup> ion in solution acts as a base to accept a proton from H<sub>3</sub>O<sup>+</sup>, forming H<sub>2</sub>O and HCl. Thus H<sub>3</sub>O<sup>+</sup> and H<sub>2</sub>O constitute a second conjugate acid--base pair. In general, any acid--base reaction must contain *two* conjugate acid--base pairs, which in this case are HCl/Cl<sup>−</sup> and H<sub>3</sub>O<sup>+</sup>/H<sub>2</sub>O.

````````````{important} Note the Pattern

All acid--base reactions contain *two* conjugate acid--base pairs.

````````````

![Chemical illustration from Chapter 16: Aqueous Acid--Base Equilibriums](/images/section_20/13307049a0af1fc2a8588ee638396963.jpg)

Similarly, in the reaction of acetic acid with water, acetic acid donates a proton to water, which acts as the base. In the reverse reaction, H<sub>3</sub>O<sup>+</sup> is the acid that donates a proton to the acetate ion, which acts as the base. Once again, we have two conjugate acid--base pairs: the parent acid and its conjugate base (CH<sub>3</sub>CO<sub>2</sub>H/CH<sub>3</sub>CO<sub>2</sub><sup>−</sup>) and the parent base and its conjugate acid (H<sub>3</sub>O<sup>+</sup>/H<sub>2</sub>O).

![Chemical illustration from Chapter 16: Aqueous Acid--Base Equilibriums](/images/section_20/0976fc4b1971263ab1f1bf651617f1a6.jpg)

In the reaction of ammonia with water to give ammonium ions and hydroxide ions ([Equation 16.3](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s01-s01-eq03)), ammonia acts as a base by accepting a proton from a water molecule, which in this case means that water is acting as an acid. In the reverse reaction, an ammonium ion acts as an acid by donating a proton to a hydroxide ion, and the hydroxide ion acts as a base. The conjugate acid--base pairs for this reaction are NH<sub>4</sub><sup>+</sup>/NH<sub>3</sub> and H<sub>2</sub>O/OH<sup>−</sup>. Some common conjugate acid--base pairs are shown in [Figure 16.2 \"The Relative Strengths of Some Common Conjugate Acid--Base Pairs\"](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s01-f01).

![Chemical illustration from Chapter 16: Aqueous Acid--Base Equilibriums](/images/section_20/d74ff3bb2b8f11ddd4804cc71e8fcb32.jpg)

(src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s01-f01)=

Figure 16.2 The Relative Strengths of Some Common Conjugate Acid--Base Pairs

![Figure 16.2 The Relative Strengths of Some Common Conjugate Acid--Base Pairs](/images/section_20/eec51e5746bb3b13108029410ecf1f45.jpg)

The strongest acids are at the bottom left, and the strongest bases are at the top right. The conjugate base of a strong acid is a very weak base, and, conversely, the conjugate acid of a strong base is a very weak acid.

### Acid--Base Equilibrium Constants: *K*<sub>a</sub>, *K*<sub>b</sub>, p*K*<sub>a</sub>, and p*K*<sub>b</sub>

The magnitude of the equilibrium constant for an ionization reaction can be used to determine the relative strengths of acids and bases. For example, the general equation for the ionization of a weak acid in water, where HA is the parent acid and A<sup>−</sup> is its conjugate base, is as follows:

Equation 16.14

$$\left. \text{HA(aq)} + \text{H}_{2}\text{O(l)}\rightleftharpoons\text{H}_{3}\text{O}^{+}\text{(aq)} + \text{A}^{-}\text{(aq)} \right.$$

The equilibrium constant for this dissociation is as follows:

(src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s02-eq02)=

Equation 16.15

$$K = \frac{\lbrack\text{H}_{3}\text{O}^{+}\rbrack\lbrack\text{A}^{-}\rbrack}{\lbrack\text{H}_{2}\text{O}\rbrack\lbrack\text{HA]}}$$

As we noted earlier, the concentration of water is essentially constant for all reactions in aqueous solution, so \[H<sub>2</sub>O\] in [Equation 16.15](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s02-eq02) can be incorporated into a new quantity, the acid ionization constant (*K*<sub>a</sub>)[^chem-4], also called the *acid dissociation constant*:

(src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s02-eq03)=

Equation 16.16

$$K_{\text{a}} = K\lbrack\text{H}_{2}\text{O}\rbrack = \frac{\lbrack\text{H}_{3}\text{O}^{+}\rbrack\lbrack\text{A}^{-}\rbrack}{\text{[HA]}}$$

Thus the numerical values of *K* and *K*<sub>a</sub> differ by the concentration of water (55.3 M). Again, for simplicity, H<sub>3</sub>O<sup>+</sup> can be written as H<sup>+</sup> in [Equation 16.16](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s02-eq03). Keep in mind, though, that free H<sup>+</sup> does not exist in aqueous solutions and that a proton is transferred to H<sub>2</sub>O in all acid ionization reactions to form H<sub>3</sub>O<sup>+</sup>. The larger the *K*<sub>a</sub>, the stronger the acid and the higher the H<sup>+</sup> concentration at equilibrium.[^chem-5] The values of *K*<sub>a</sub> for a number of common acids are given in [Table 16.2 \"Values of \"](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s02-t01).

(src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s02-t01)=

Weak bases react with water to produce the hydroxide ion, as shown in the following general equation, where B is the parent base and BH<sup>+</sup> is its conjugate acid:

Equation 16.17

$$\left. \text{B(aq)} + \text{H}_{2}\text{O(l)}\rightleftharpoons\text{BH}^{+}\text{(aq)} + \text{OH}^{-}\text{(aq)} \right.$$

The equilibrium constant for this reaction is the base ionization constant (*K*<sub>b</sub>)[^chem-6], also called the *base dissociation constant*:

(src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s02-eq05)=

Equation 16.18

$$K_{\text{b}} = K\lbrack\text{H}_{2}\text{O}\rbrack = \frac{\lbrack\text{BH}^{+}\rbrack\lbrack\text{OH}^{-}\rbrack}{\text{[B]}}$$

Once again, the concentration of water is constant, so it does not appear in the equilibrium constant expression; instead, it is included in the *K*<sub>b</sub>. The larger the *K*<sub>b</sub>, the stronger the base and the higher the OH<sup>−</sup> concentration at equilibrium. The values of *K*<sub>b</sub> for a number of common weak bases are given in [Table 16.3 \"Values of \"](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s02-t02).
