There is a simple relationship between the magnitude of *K*<sub>a</sub> for an acid and *K*<sub>b</sub> for its conjugate base. Consider, for example, the ionization of hydrocyanic acid (HCN) in water to produce an acidic solution, and the reaction of CN<sup>−</sup> with water to produce a basic solution:

(src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s02-eq06)=

Equation 16.19

$$\left. \text{HCN(aq)}\rightleftharpoons\text{H}^{+}\text{(aq)} + \text{CN}^{-}\text{(aq)} \right.$$

(src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s02-eq07)=

Equation 16.20

$$\left. \text{CN}^{-}\text{(aq)} + \text{H}_{2}\text{O(l)}\rightleftharpoons\text{OH}^{-}\text{(aq)} + \text{HCN(aq)} \right.$$

The equilibrium constant expression for the ionization of HCN is as follows:

Equation 16.21

$$K_{\text{a}} = \frac{\lbrack\text{H}^{+}\rbrack\lbrack\text{CN}^{-}\rbrack}{\text{[HCN]}}$$

The corresponding expression for the reaction of cyanide with water is as follows:

Equation 16.22

$$K_{\text{b}} = \frac{\lbrack\text{OH}^{-}\rbrack\text{[HCN]}}{\lbrack\text{CN}^{-}\rbrack}$$

If we add [Equation 16.19](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s02-eq06) and [Equation 16.20](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s02-eq07), we obtain the following (recall from [Chapter 15 \"Chemical Equilibrium\"](/chapters/ch-15-chemical-equilibrium.md#src-averill-1-0-ch15) that the equilibrium constant for the *sum* of two reactions is the *product* of the equilibrium constants for the individual reactions):

$$\begin{array}{rc}
\left. \cancel{\text{HCN}}\text{(aq)}\rightleftharpoons\text{H}^{+}\text{(aq)} + \cancel{\text{CN}^{-}\text{(aq)}} \right. & {K_{\text{a}} = \lbrack\text{H}^{+}\rbrack\cancel{\lbrack\text{CN}^{-}\rbrack}\text{/}\cancel{\text{[HCN]}}} \\
\left. \cancel{\text{CN}^{-}\text{(aq)}} + \text{H}_{2}\text{O(l)}\rightleftharpoons\text{OH}^{-}\text{(aq)} + \cancel{\text{HCN(aq)}} \right. & {K_{\text{b}} = \lbrack\text{OH}^{-}\rbrack\cancel{\text{[HCN]}}\text{/}\cancel{\lbrack\text{CN}^{-}\rbrack}} \\
\left. \text{H}_{2}\text{O(l)}\rightleftharpoons\text{H}^{+}\text{(aq)} + \text{OH}^{-}\text{(aq)} \right. & {K = K_{\text{a}} \times K_{\text{b}} = \lbrack\text{H}^{+}\rbrack\lbrack\text{OH}^{-}\rbrack}
\end{array}$$

In this case, the sum of the reactions described by *K*<sub>a</sub> and *K*<sub>b</sub> is the equation for the autoionization of water, and the product of the two equilibrium constants is *K*<sub>w</sub>:

(src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s02-eq10)=

Equation 16.23

*K*<sub>a</sub>*K*<sub>b</sub> = *K*<sub>w</sub>

Thus if we know either *K*<sub>a</sub> for an acid or *K*<sub>b</sub> for its conjugate base, we can calculate the other equilibrium constant for any conjugate acid--base pair.

Just as with pH, pOH, and p*K*<sub>w</sub>, we can use negative logarithms to avoid exponential notation in writing acid and base ionization constants, by defining p*K*<sub>a</sub> as follows:

(src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s02-eq11)=

Equation 16.24

p*K*<sub>a</sub> = −log<sub>10</sub>*K*<sub>a</sub>

(src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s02-eq12)=

Equation 16.25

$$K_{\text{a}} = 10^{- \text{p}K_{\text{a}}}$$

and p*K*<sub>b</sub> as

(src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s02-eq13)=

Equation 16.26

p*K*<sub>b</sub> = −log<sub>10</sub>*K*<sub>b</sub>

(src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s02-eq14)=

Equation 16.27

$$K_{\text{b}} = 10^{- \text{p}K_{\text{b}}}$$

Similarly, [Equation 16.23](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s02-eq10), which expresses the relationship between *K*<sub>a</sub> and *K*<sub>b</sub>, can be written in logarithmic form as follows:

(src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s02-eq15)=

Equation 16.28

p*K*<sub>a</sub> + p*K*<sub>b</sub> = p*K*<sub>w</sub>

At 25°C, this becomes

(src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s02-eq16)=

Equation 16.29

p*K*<sub>a</sub> + p*K*<sub>b</sub> = 14.00

The values of p*K*<sub>a</sub> and p*K*<sub>b</sub> are given for several common acids and bases in [Table 16.2 \"Values of \"](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s02-t01) and [Table 16.3 \"Values of \"](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s02-t02), respectively, and a more extensive set of data is provided in [Chapter 27 \"Appendix C: Dissociation Constants and p\"](/appendices/appendix-c-dissociation-consta.md#src-averill-1-0-ch96appc) and [Chapter 28 \"Appendix D: Dissociation Constants and p\"](/appendices/appendix-d-dissociation-consta.md#src-averill-1-0-ch96appd). Because of the use of negative logarithms, smaller values of p*K*<sub>a</sub> correspond to larger acid ionization constants and hence stronger acids. For example, nitrous acid (HNO<sub>2</sub>), with a p*K*<sub>a</sub> of 3.25, is about a 1000 times stronger acid than hydrocyanic acid (HCN), with a p*K*<sub>a</sub> of 9.21. Conversely, smaller values of p*K*<sub>b</sub> correspond to larger base ionization constants and hence stronger bases.

The relative strengths of some common acids and their conjugate bases are shown graphically in [Figure 16.2 \"The Relative Strengths of Some Common Conjugate Acid--Base Pairs\"](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s01-f01). The conjugate acid--base pairs are listed in order (from top to bottom) of increasing acid strength, which corresponds to decreasing values of p*K*<sub>a</sub>. This order corresponds to decreasing strength of the conjugate base or increasing values of p*K*<sub>b</sub>. At the bottom left of [Figure 16.2 \"The Relative Strengths of Some Common Conjugate Acid--Base Pairs\"](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s01-f01) are the common strong acids; at the top right are the most common strong bases. Notice the inverse relationship between the strength of the parent acid and the strength of the conjugate base. Thus the conjugate base of a strong acid is a very weak base, and the conjugate base of a very weak acid is a strong base.

````````````{important} Note the Pattern

The conjugate base of a strong acid is a weak base and vice versa.

````````````

We can use the relative strengths of acids and bases to predict the direction of an acid--base reaction by following a single rule: *an acid--base equilibrium always favors the side with the weaker acid and base, as indicated by these arrows*:

![Chemical illustration from Chapter 16: Aqueous Acid--Base Equilibriums](/images/section_20/bd4db789e7fecd8a05a4f274ad96b6f6.jpg)

In an acid--base reaction, the proton always reacts with the stronger base.

For example, hydrochloric acid is a strong acid that ionizes essentially completely in dilute aqueous solution to produce H<sub>3</sub>O<sup>+</sup> and Cl<sup>−</sup>; only negligible amounts of HCl molecules remain undissociated. Hence the ionization equilibrium lies virtually all the way to the right, as represented by a single arrow:

Equation 16.30

$$\left. \text{HCl(aq)}\text{~+~}\text{H}_{2}\text{O(l)}\rightarrow\text{H}_{3}\text{O}^{+}\text{(aq)} + \text{Cl}^{-}\text{(aq)} \right.$$

In contrast, acetic acid is a weak acid, and water is a weak base. Consequently, aqueous solutions of acetic acid contain mostly acetic acid molecules in equilibrium with a small concentration of H<sub>3</sub>O<sup>+</sup> and acetate ions, and the ionization equilibrium lies far to the left, as represented by these arrows:

Figure 16.3

![Figure 16.3](/images/section_20/d98649471847a012b4ccef5481f06775.jpg)

 

Similarly, in the reaction of ammonia with water, the hydroxide ion is a strong base, and ammonia is a weak base, whereas the ammonium ion is a stronger acid than water. Hence this equilibrium also lies to the left:

Figure 16.4

![Figure 16.4](/images/section_20/1418edf1ed4c6e6213e768b770d4280b.jpg)

 

````````````{important} Note the Pattern

All acid--base equilibriums favor the side with the weaker acid and base. Thus the proton is bound to the stronger base.

````````````

### Example 2

1.  Calculate *K*<sub>b</sub> and p*K*<sub>b</sub> of the butyrate ion (CH<sub>3</sub>CH<sub>2</sub>CH<sub>2</sub>CO<sub>2</sub><sup>−</sup>). The p*K*<sub>a</sub> of butyric acid at 25°C is 4.83. Butyric acid is responsible for the foul smell of rancid butter.
2.  Calculate *K*<sub>a</sub> and p*K*<sub>a</sub> of the dimethylammonium ion \[(CH<sub>3</sub>)<sub>2</sub>NH<sub>2</sub><sup>+</sup>\]. The base ionization constant *K*<sub>b</sub> of dimethylamine \[(CH<sub>3</sub>)<sub>2</sub>NH\] is 5.4 × 10<sup>−4</sup> at 25°C.

**Given:** p*K*<sub>a</sub> and *K*<sub>b</sub>

**Asked for:** corresponding *K*<sub>b</sub> and p*K*<sub>b</sub>, *K*<sub>a</sub> and p*K*<sub>a</sub>

**Strategy:**

The constants *K*<sub>a</sub> and *K*<sub>b</sub> are related as shown in [Equation 16.23](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s02-eq10). The p*K*<sub>a</sub> and p*K*<sub>b</sub> for an acid and its conjugate base are related as shown in [Equation 16.28](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s02-eq15) and [Equation 16.29](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s02-eq16). Use the relationships p*K* = −log *K* and *K* = 10<sup>−p*K*</sup> ([Equation 16.24](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s02-eq11) and [Equation 16.26](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s02-eq13)) to convert between *K*<sub>a</sub> and p*K*<sub>a</sub> or *K*<sub>b</sub> and p*K*<sub>b</sub>.

**Solution:**

1.  We are given the p*K*<sub>a</sub> for butyric acid and asked to calculate the *K*<sub>b</sub> and the p*K*<sub>b</sub> for its conjugate base, the butyrate ion. Because the p*K*<sub>a</sub> value cited is for a temperature of 25°C, we can use [Equation 16.29](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s02-eq16): p*K*<sub>a</sub> + p*K*<sub>b</sub> = p*K*<sub>w</sub> = 14.00. Substituting the p*K*<sub>a</sub> and solving for the p*K*<sub>b</sub>,

    $$\begin{aligned}
    {4.83 + \text{p}K_{\text{b}}} & {= 14.00} \\
    {\text{p}K_{b}} & {= 14.00 - 4.83 = 9.17}
    \end{aligned}$$

    Because p*K*<sub>b</sub> = −log *K*<sub>b</sub>, *K*<sub>b</sub> is 10<sup>−9.17</sup> = 6.8 × 10<sup>−10</sup>.

2.  In this case, we are given *K*<sub>b</sub> for a base (dimethylamine) and asked to calculate *K*<sub>a</sub> and p*K*<sub>a</sub> for its conjugate acid, the dimethylammonium ion. Because the initial quantity given is *K*<sub>b</sub> rather than p*K*<sub>b</sub>, we can use [Equation 16.23](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s02-eq10): *K*<sub>a</sub>*K*<sub>b</sub> = *K*<sub>w</sub>. Substituting the values of *K*<sub>b</sub> and *K*<sub>w</sub> at 25°C and solving for *K*<sub>a</sub>,

    $$\begin{aligned}
    {K_{\text{a}}(5.4 \times 10^{- 4})} & {= 1.01 \times 10^{- 14}} \\
    K_{\text{a}} & {= 1.9 \times 10^{- 11}}
    \end{aligned}$$

    Because p*K*<sub>a</sub> = −log *K*<sub>a</sub>, we have p*K*<sub>a</sub> = −log(1.9 × 10<sup>−11</sup>) = 10.72. We could also have converted *K*<sub>b</sub> to p*K*<sub>b</sub> to obtain the same answer:

    $$\begin{aligned}
    {\text{p}K_{\text{b}}} & {= - \log(5.4 \times 10^{- 4}) = 3.27} \\
    {\text{p}K_{\text{a}} + \text{p}K_{\text{b}}} & {= 14.00} \\
    {\text{p}K_{\text{a}}} & {= 10.73} \\
    K_{\text{a}} & {= 10^{- \text{p}K_{\text{a}}} = 10^{- 10.73} = 1.9 \times 10^{- 11}}
    \end{aligned}$$

    If we are given any one of these four quantities for an acid or a base (*K*<sub>a</sub>, p*K*<sub>a</sub>, *K*<sub>b</sub>, or p*K*<sub>b</sub>), we can calculate the other three.

Exercise

Lactic acid \[CH<sub>3</sub>CH(OH)CO<sub>2</sub>H\] is responsible for the pungent taste and smell of sour milk; it is also thought to produce soreness in fatigued muscles. Its p*K*<sub>a</sub> is 3.86 at 25°C. Calculate *K*<sub>a</sub> for lactic acid and p*K*<sub>b</sub> and *K*<sub>b</sub> for the lactate ion.

**Answer:** *K*<sub>a</sub> = 1.4 × 10<sup>−4</sup> for lactic acid; p*K*<sub>b</sub> = 10.14 and *K*<sub>b</sub> = 7.2 × 10<sup>−11</sup> for the lactate ion

### Solutions of Strong Acids and Bases: The Leveling Effect

You will notice in [Table 16.2 \"Values of \"](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s02-t01) that acids like H<sub>2</sub>SO<sub>4</sub> and HNO<sub>3</sub> lie *above* the hydronium ion, meaning that they have p*K*<sub>a</sub> values less than zero and are stronger acids than the H<sub>3</sub>O<sup>+</sup> ion.[^chem-7] In fact, all six of the common strong acids that we first encountered in [Chapter 4 \"Reactions in Aqueous Solution\"](/chapters/ch-04-reactions-in-aqueous-solution.md#src-averill-1-0-ch04) have p*K*<sub>a</sub> values less than zero, which means that they have a greater tendency to lose a proton than does the H<sub>3</sub>O<sup>+</sup> ion. Conversely, the conjugate bases of these strong acids are weaker bases than water. Consequently, the proton-transfer equilibriums for these strong acids lie far to the right, and adding any of the common strong acids to water results in an essentially stoichiometric reaction of the acid with water to form a solution of the H<sub>3</sub>O<sup>+</sup> ion and the conjugate base of the acid.

Although *K*<sub>a</sub> for HI is about 10<sup>8</sup> greater than *K*<sub>a</sub> for HNO<sub>3</sub>, the reaction of either HI or HNO<sub>3</sub> with water gives an essentially stoichiometric solution of H<sub>3</sub>O<sup>+</sup> and I<sup>−</sup> or NO<sub>3</sub><sup>−</sup>. In fact, a 0.1 M aqueous solution of any strong acid actually contains 0.1 M H<sub>3</sub>O<sup>+</sup>, *regardless of the identity of the strong acid*. This phenomenon is called the leveling effect[^chem-8]: any species that is a stronger acid than the conjugate acid of water (H<sub>3</sub>O<sup>+</sup>) is leveled to the strength of H<sub>3</sub>O<sup>+</sup> in aqueous solution because H<sub>3</sub>O<sup>+</sup> is the strongest acid that can exist in equilibrium with water. Consequently, it is impossible to distinguish between the strengths of acids such as HI and HNO<sub>3</sub> in aqueous solution, and an alternative approach must be used to determine their relative acid strengths.

One method is to use a solvent such as anhydrous acetic acid. Because acetic acid is a stronger acid than water, it must also be a weaker base, with a lesser tendency to accept a proton than H<sub>2</sub>O. Measurements of the conductivity of 0.1 M solutions of both HI and HNO<sub>3</sub> in acetic acid show that HI is completely dissociated, but HNO<sub>3</sub> is only partially dissociated and behaves like a weak acid *in this solvent*. This result clearly tells us that HI is a stronger acid than HNO<sub>3</sub>. The relative order of acid strengths and approximate *K*<sub>a</sub> and p*K*<sub>a</sub> values for the strong acids at the top of [Table 16.2 \"Values of \"](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s02-t01) were determined using measurements like this and different nonaqueous solvents.

````````````{important} Note the Pattern

In aqueous solutions, \[H<sub>3</sub>O<sup>+</sup>\] is the strongest acid and OH<sup>−</sup> is the strongest base that can exist in equilibrium with H<sub>2</sub>O.

````````````

The leveling effect applies to solutions of strong bases as well: In aqueous solution, any base stronger than OH<sup>−</sup> is leveled to the strength of OH<sup>−</sup> because OH<sup>−</sup> is the strongest base that can exist in equilibrium with water. Salts such as K<sub>2</sub>O, NaOCH<sub>3</sub> (sodium methoxide), and NaNH<sub>2</sub> (sodamide, or sodium amide), whose anions are the conjugate bases of species that would lie below water in [Table 16.3 \"Values of \"](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s02-t02), are all strong bases that react essentially completely (and often violently) with water, accepting a proton to give a solution of OH<sup>−</sup> and the corresponding cation:

Equation 16.31

$$\left. \text{K}_{2}\text{O(s)} + \text{H}_{2}\text{O(l)}\rightarrow 2\text{OH}^{-}\text{(aq)} + 2\text{K}^{+}\text{(aq)} \right.$$

Equation 16.32

$$\left. \text{NaOCH}_{3}\text{(s)} + \text{H}_{2}\text{O(l)}\rightarrow\text{OH}^{-}\text{(aq)} + \text{Na}^{+}\text{(aq)} + \text{CH}_{3}\text{OH(aq)} \right.$$

Equation 16.33

$$\left. \text{NaNH}_{2}\text{(s)} + \text{H}_{2}\text{O(l)}\rightarrow\text{OH}^{-}\text{(aq)} + \text{Na}^{+}\text{(aq)} + \text{NH}_{3}\text{(aq)} \right.$$

Other examples that you may encounter are potassium hydride (KH) and organometallic compounds such as methyl lithium (CH<sub>3</sub>Li).

### Polyprotic Acids and Bases

As you learned in [Chapter 4 \"Reactions in Aqueous Solution\"](/chapters/ch-04-reactions-in-aqueous-solution.md#src-averill-1-0-ch04), *polyprotic acids* such as H<sub>2</sub>SO<sub>4</sub>, H<sub>3</sub>PO<sub>4</sub>, and H<sub>2</sub>CO<sub>3</sub> contain more than one ionizable proton, and the protons are lost in a stepwise manner. The fully protonated species is always the strongest acid because it is easier to remove a proton from a neutral molecule than from a negatively charged ion. Thus acid strength decreases with the loss of subsequent protons, and, correspondingly, the p*K*<sub>a</sub> increases. Consider H<sub>2</sub>SO<sub>4</sub>, for example:

Figure 16.5

![Figure 16.5](/images/section_20/3f2430dc46ca197d55635a0334f5ae11.jpg)

 

Equation 16.34

$$\begin{array}{rc}
\left. \text{HSO}_{4}{}^{-}\text{(aq)}\rightleftharpoons\text{SO}_{4}{}^{2 -}\text{(aq)} + \text{H}^{+}\text{(aq)} \right. & {\text{p}K_{\text{a}} = 1.99}
\end{array}$$

The equilibrium in the first reaction lies far to the right, consistent with H<sub>2</sub>SO<sub>4</sub> being a strong acid. In contrast, in the second reaction, appreciable quantities of both HSO<sub>4</sub><sup>−</sup> and SO<sub>4</sub><sup>2−</sup> are present at equilibrium.

````````````{important} Note the Pattern

For a polyprotic acid, acid strength decreases and the p*K*<sub>a</sub> increases with the sequential loss of each proton.

````````````

The hydrogen sulfate ion (HSO<sub>4</sub><sup>−</sup>) is both the conjugate base of H<sub>2</sub>SO<sub>4</sub> and the conjugate acid of SO<sub>4</sub><sup>2−</sup>. Just like water, HSO<sub>4</sub><sup>−</sup> can therefore act as *either* an acid or a base, depending on whether the other reactant is a stronger acid or a stronger base. Conversely, the sulfate ion (SO<sub>4</sub><sup>2−</sup>) is a *polyprotic base* that is capable of accepting two protons in a stepwise manner:

(src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s04-eq02)=

Figure 16.6

![Figure 16.6](/images/section_20/d50e85974117226e513f9e559c0c6ced.jpg)

 

(src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s04-eq03)=

Figure 16.7

![Figure 16.7](/images/section_20/860881f17b7e176a132c82b3816ae598.jpg)

 

Like any other conjugate acid--base pair, the strengths of the conjugate acids and bases are related by p*K*<sub>a</sub> + p*K*<sub>b</sub> = p*K*<sub>w</sub>. Consider, for example, the HSO<sub>4</sub><sup>−</sup>/ SO<sub>4</sub><sup>2−</sup> conjugate acid--base pair. From [Table 16.2 \"Values of \"](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s02-t01), we see that the p*K*<sub>a</sub> of HSO<sub>4</sub><sup>−</sup> is 1.99. Hence the p*K*<sub>b</sub> of SO<sub>4</sub><sup>2−</sup> is 14.00 − 1.99 = 12.01. Thus sulfate is a rather weak base, whereas OH<sup>−</sup> is a strong base, so the equilibrium shown in [Figure 16.6](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s04-eq02) lies to the left. The HSO<sub>4</sub><sup>−</sup> ion is also a very weak base \[p*K*<sub>a</sub> of H<sub>2</sub>SO<sub>4</sub> = 2.0, p*K*<sub>b</sub> of HSO<sub>4</sub><sup>−</sup> = 14 − (−2.0) = 16\], which is consistent with what we expect for the conjugate base of a strong acid. Thus the equilibrium shown in [Figure 16.7](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s04-eq03) also lies almost completely to the left. Once again, equilibrium favors the formation of the weaker acid--base pair.

### Example 3

Predict whether the equilibrium for each reaction lies to the left or the right as written.

1.  $\left. \text{NH}_{4}{}^{+}\text{(aq)} + \text{PO}_{4}{}^{3 -}\text{(aq)}\rightleftharpoons\text{NH}_{3}\text{(aq)} + \text{HPO}_{4}{}^{2 -}\text{(aq)} \right.$
2.  $\left. \text{CH}_{3}\text{CH}_{2}\text{CO}_{2}\text{H(aq)} + \text{CN}^{-}\text{(aq)}\rightleftharpoons\text{CH}_{3}\text{CH}_{2}\text{CO}_{2}{}^{-}\text{(aq)} + \text{HCN(aq)} \right.$

**Given:** balanced chemical equation

**Asked for:** equilibrium position

**Strategy:**

Identify the conjugate acid--base pairs in each reaction. Then refer to [Table 16.2 \"Values of \"](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s02-t01), [Table 16.3 \"Values of \"](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s02-t02), and [Figure 16.2 \"The Relative Strengths of Some Common Conjugate Acid--Base Pairs\"](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s01-f01) to determine which is the stronger acid and base. Equilibrium always favors the formation of the weaker acid--base pair.

**Solution:**

1.  The conjugate acid--base pairs are NH<sub>4</sub><sup>+</sup>/NH<sub>3</sub> and HPO<sub>4</sub><sup>2−</sup>/PO<sub>4</sub><sup>3−</sup>. According to [Table 16.2 \"Values of \"](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s02-t01) and [Table 16.3 \"Values of \"](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s02-t02), NH<sub>4</sub><sup>+</sup> is a stronger acid (p*K*<sub>a</sub> = 9.25) than HPO<sub>4</sub><sup>2−</sup> (p*K*<sub>a</sub> = 12.32), and PO<sub>4</sub><sup>3−</sup> is a stronger base (p*K*<sub>b</sub> = 1.68) than NH<sub>3</sub> (p*K*<sub>b</sub> = 4.75). The equilibrium will therefore lie to the right, favoring the formation of the weaker acid--base pair:

    ![Chemical illustration from Chapter 16: Aqueous Acid--Base Equilibriums](/images/section_20/e10ca2d1c7852ba9f6ff27b50994ddb2.jpg)

     

2.  The conjugate acid--base pairs are CH<sub>3</sub>CH<sub>2</sub>CO<sub>2</sub>H/CH<sub>3</sub>CH<sub>2</sub>CO<sub>2</sub><sup>−</sup> and HCN/CN<sup>−</sup>. According to [Table 16.2 \"Values of \"](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s02-t01), HCN is a weak acid (p*K*<sub>a</sub> = 9.21) and CN<sup>−</sup> is a moderately weak base (p*K*<sub>b</sub> = 4.79). Propionic acid (CH<sub>3</sub>CH<sub>2</sub>CO<sub>2</sub>H) is not listed in [Table 16.2 \"Values of \"](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s02-t01), however. In a situation like this, the best approach is to look for a similar compound whose acid--base properties *are* listed. For example, propionic acid and acetic acid are identical except for the groups attached to the carbon atom of the carboxylic acid (−CH<sub>2</sub>CH<sub>3</sub> versus −CH<sub>3</sub>), so we might expect the two compounds to have similar acid--base properties. In particular, we would expect the p*K*<sub>a</sub> of propionic acid to be similar in magnitude to the p*K*<sub>a</sub> of acetic acid. (In fact, the p*K*<sub>a</sub> of propionic acid is 4.87, compared to 4.76 for acetic acid, which makes propionic acid a slightly weaker acid than acetic acid.) Thus propionic acid should be a significantly stronger acid than HCN. Because the stronger acid forms the weaker conjugate base, we predict that cyanide will be a stronger base than propionate. The equilibrium will therefore lie to the right, favoring the formation of the weaker acid--base pair:

    ![Chemical illustration from Chapter 16: Aqueous Acid--Base Equilibriums](/images/section_20/adadbce193f4b702f22c2f57f7788950.jpg)

Exercise

Predict whether the equilibrium for each reaction lies to the left or the right as written.

1.  $\left. \text{H}_{2}\text{O(l)} + \text{HS}^{-}\text{(aq)}\rightleftharpoons\text{OH}^{-}\text{(aq)} + \text{H}_{2}\text{S(aq)} \right.$
2.  $\left. \text{HCO}_{2}{}^{-}\text{(aq)} + \text{HSO}_{4}{}^{-}\text{(aq)}\rightleftharpoons\text{HCO}_{2}\text{H(aq)} + \text{SO}_{4}{}^{2 -}\text{(aq)} \right.$

**Answer:**

1.  left
2.  left

### Acid--Base Properties of Solutions of Salts

We can also use the relative strengths of conjugate acid--base pairs to understand the acid--base properties of solutions of salts. In [Chapter 4 \"Reactions in Aqueous Solution\"](/chapters/ch-04-reactions-in-aqueous-solution.md#src-averill-1-0-ch04), you learned that a *neutralization reaction* can be defined as the reaction of an acid and a base to produce a salt and water. That is, another cation, such as Na<sup>+</sup>, replaces the proton on the acid. An example is the reaction of CH<sub>3</sub>CO<sub>2</sub>H, a weak acid, with NaOH, a strong base:

Equation 16.35

$$\underset{\text{acid}}{\text{CH}_{\text{3}}\text{CO}_{\text{2}}\text{H(l)~}} + \underset{\text{base}}{\text{NaOH(s)}}\overset{\text{H}_{2}\text{O}}{\rightarrow}\underset{\text{salt}}{\text{CH}_{3}\text{CO}_{2}\text{Na(aq)~}} + \underset{\text{water}}{\text{H}_{2}\text{O(l)}}$$

Depending on the acid--base properties of its component ions, however, a salt can dissolve in water to produce a neutral solution, a basic solution, or an acidic solution.

When a salt such as NaCl dissolves in water, it produces Na<sup>+</sup>(aq) and Cl<sup>−</sup>(aq) ions. Using a Lewis approach, the Na<sup>+</sup> ion can be viewed as an acid because it is an electron pair acceptor, although its low charge and relatively large radius make it a very weak acid. The Cl<sup>−</sup> ion is the conjugate base of the strong acid HCl, so it has essentially no basic character. Consequently, dissolving NaCl in water has no effect on the pH of a solution, and the solution remains neutral.

Now let\'s compare this behavior to the behavior of aqueous solutions of potassium cyanide and sodium acetate. Again, the cations (K<sup>+</sup> and Na<sup>+</sup>) have essentially no acidic character, but the anions (CN<sup>−</sup> and CH<sub>3</sub>CO<sub>2</sub><sup>−</sup>) are weak bases that can react with water because they are the conjugate bases of the weak acids HCN and acetic acid, respectively.

Figure 16.8

![Figure 16.8](/images/section_20/3915c7352e2c61241a7dfd8ef207a08c.jpg)

 

(src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s05-eq03)=

Figure 16.9

![Figure 16.9](/images/section_20/32e234e7e5427cf3447063e5de4989dc.jpg)

 

Neither reaction proceeds very far to the right as written because the formation of the weaker acid--base pair is favored. Both HCN and acetic acid are stronger acids than water, and hydroxide is a stronger base than either acetate or cyanide, so in both cases, the equilibrium lies to the left. Nonetheless, each of these reactions generates enough hydroxide ions to produce a basic solution. For example, the pH of a 0.1 M solution of sodium acetate or potassium cyanide at 25°C is 8.8 or 11.1, respectively. From [Table 16.2 \"Values of \"](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s02-t01) and [Figure 16.2 \"The Relative Strengths of Some Common Conjugate Acid--Base Pairs\"](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s01-f01), we can see that CN<sup>−</sup> is a stronger base (p*K*<sub>b</sub> = 4.79) than acetate (p*K*<sub>b</sub> = 9.24), which is consistent with KCN producing a more basic solution than sodium acetate at the same concentration.

In contrast, the conjugate acid of a weak base should be a weak acid ([Figure 16.2 \"The Relative Strengths of Some Common Conjugate Acid--Base Pairs\"](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s01-f01)). For example, ammonium chloride and pyridinium chloride are salts produced by reacting ammonia and pyridine, respectively, with HCl. As you already know, the chloride ion is such a weak base that it does not react with water. In contrast, the cations of the two salts are weak acids that react with water as follows:

(src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s05-eq04)=

Figure 16.10

![Figure 16.10](/images/section_20/4a509d4d27b946cd643fc195142fbc2e.jpg)

 

Figure 16.11

![Figure 16.11](/images/section_20/533c2455e62e5e3ea775f43b4d0b2eeb.jpg)

 

[Figure 16.2 \"The Relative Strengths of Some Common Conjugate Acid--Base Pairs\"](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s01-f01) shows that H<sub>3</sub>O<sup>+</sup> is a stronger acid than either NH<sub>4</sub><sup>+</sup> or C<sub>5</sub>H<sub>5</sub>NH<sup>+</sup>, and conversely, ammonia and pyridine are both stronger bases than water. The equilibrium will therefore lie far to the left in both cases, favoring the weaker acid--base pair. The H<sub>3</sub>O<sup>+</sup> concentration produced by the reactions is great enough, however, to decrease the pH of the solution significantly: the pH of a 0.10 M solution of ammonium chloride or pyridinium chloride at 25°C is 5.13 or 3.12, respectively. This is consistent with the information shown in [Figure 16.2 \"The Relative Strengths of Some Common Conjugate Acid--Base Pairs\"](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s01-f01), indicating that the pyridinium ion is more acidic than the ammonium ion.

What happens with aqueous solutions of a salt such as ammonium acetate, where both the cation *and* the anion can react separately with water to produce an acid and a base, respectively? According to [Figure 16.10](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s05-eq04), the ammonium ion will lower the pH, while according to [Figure 16.9](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s05-eq03), the acetate ion will raise the pH. This particular case is unusual, in that the cation is as strong an acid as the anion is a base (p*K*<sub>a</sub> ≈ p*K*<sub>b</sub>). Consequently, the two effects cancel, and the solution remains neutral. With salts in which the cation is a stronger acid than the anion is a base, the final solution has a pH \< 7.00. Conversely, if the cation is a weaker acid than the anion is a base, the final solution has a pH \> 7.00.

Solutions of simple salts of metal ions can also be acidic, even though a metal ion cannot donate a proton directly to water to produce H<sub>3</sub>O<sup>+</sup>. Instead, a metal ion can act as a Lewis acid and interact with water, a Lewis base, by coordinating to a lone pair of electrons on the oxygen atom to form a hydrated metal ion (part (a) in [Figure 16.12 \"Effect of a Metal Ion on the Acidity of Water\"](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s05-f01)), as discussed in [Chapter 4 \"Reactions in Aqueous Solution\"](/chapters/ch-04-reactions-in-aqueous-solution.md#src-averill-1-0-ch04). A water molecule coordinated to a metal ion is more acidic than a free water molecule for two reasons. First, repulsive electrostatic interactions between the positively charged metal ion and the partially positively charged hydrogen atoms of the coordinated water molecule make it easier for the coordinated water to lose a proton.

![Chemical illustration from Chapter 16: Aqueous Acid--Base Equilibriums](/images/section_20/b6d354f5baefcb11543469e06c0a47ff.jpg)

Second, the positive charge on the Al<sup>3+</sup> ion attracts electron density from the oxygen atoms of the water molecules, which decreases the electron density in the O--H bonds, as shown in part (b) in [Figure 16.12 \"Effect of a Metal Ion on the Acidity of Water\"](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s05-f01). With less electron density between the O atoms and the H atoms, the O--H bonds are weaker than in a free H<sub>2</sub>O molecule, making it easier to lose a H<sup>+</sup> ion.

(src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s05-f01)=

Figure 16.12 Effect of a Metal Ion on the Acidity of Water

![Figure 16.12 Effect of a Metal Ion on the Acidity of Water](/images/section_20/cd0e612043ae85f5c1b619e49c829be9.jpg)

\(a\) Reaction of the metal ion Al<sup>3+</sup> with water to form the hydrated metal ion is an example of a Lewis acid--base reaction. (b) The positive charge on the aluminum ion attracts electron density from the oxygen atoms, which shifts electron density away from the O--H bonds. The decrease in electron density weakens the O--H bonds in the water molecules and makes it easier for them to lose a proton.

The magnitude of this effect depends on the following two factors ([Figure 16.13 \"The Effect of the Charge and Radius of a Metal Ion on the Acidity of a Coordinated Water Molecule\"](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s05-f02)):

1.  **The** *charge* **on the metal ion.** A divalent ion (M<sup>2+</sup>) has approximately twice as strong an effect on the electron density in a coordinated water molecule as a monovalent ion (M<sup>+</sup>) of the same radius.
2.  **The** *radius* **of the metal ion.** For metal ions with the same charge, the smaller the ion, the shorter the internuclear distance to the oxygen atom of the water molecule and the greater the effect of the metal on the electron density distribution in the water molecule.

(src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s05-f02)=

Figure 16.13 The Effect of the Charge and Radius of a Metal Ion on the Acidity of a Coordinated Water Molecule

![Figure 16.13 The Effect of the Charge and Radius of a Metal Ion on the Acidity of a Coordinated Water Molecule](/images/section_20/ccc1cbbe597dfda4813d5854f16e76b8.jpg)

The contours show the electron density on the O atoms and the H atoms in both a free water molecule (left) and water molecules coordinated to Na<sup>+</sup>, Mg<sup>2+</sup>, and Al<sup>3+</sup> ions. These contour maps demonstrate that the smallest, most highly charged metal ion (Al<sup>3+</sup>) causes the greatest decrease in electron density of the O--H bonds of the water molecule. Due to this effect, the acidity of hydrated metal ions increases as the charge on the metal ion increases and its radius decreases.

Thus aqueous solutions of small, highly charged metal ions, such as Al<sup>3+</sup> and Fe<sup>3+</sup>, are acidic:

Equation 16.36

$$\left. {\lbrack\text{Al}{(\text{H}_{2}\text{O})}_{6}\rbrack}^{3 +}\left( \text{aq} \right)\rightleftharpoons{\lbrack\text{Al}{(\text{H}_{2}\text{O})}_{5}\text{(OH)}\rbrack}^{2 +}\text{(aq)} + \text{H}^{+}\text{(aq)} \right.$$

The \[Al(H<sub>2</sub>O)<sub>6</sub>\]<sup>3+</sup> ion has a p*K*<sub>a</sub> of 5.0, making it almost as strong an acid as acetic acid. Because of the two factors described previously, the most important parameter for predicting the effect of a metal ion on the acidity of coordinated water molecules is the *charge-to-radius ratio* of the metal ion. A number of pairs of metal ions that lie on a diagonal line in the periodic table, such as Li<sup>+</sup> and Mg<sup>2+</sup> or Ca<sup>2+</sup> and Y<sup>3+</sup>, have different sizes and charges but similar charge-to-radius ratios. As a result, these pairs of metal ions have similar effects on the acidity of coordinated water molecules, and they often exhibit other significant similarities in chemistry as well.

````````````{important} Note the Pattern

Solutions of small, highly charged metal ions in water are acidic.

````````````

Reactions such as those discussed in this section, in which a salt reacts with water to give an acidic or basic solution, are often called hydrolysis reactions[^chem-9]. Using a separate name for this type of reaction is unfortunate because it suggests that they are somehow different. In fact, hydrolysis reactions are just acid--base reactions in which the acid is a cation or the base is an anion; they obey the same principles and rules as all other acid--base reactions.

````````````{important} Note the Pattern

A hydrolysis reaction is an acid--base reaction.

````````````

### Example 4

Predict whether aqueous solutions of these compounds are acidic, basic, or neutral.

1.  KNO<sub>3</sub>
2.  CrBr<sub>3</sub>·6H<sub>2</sub>O
3.  Na<sub>2</sub>SO<sub>4</sub>

**Given:** compound

**Asked for:** acidity or basicity of aqueous solution

**Strategy:**

**A** Assess the acid--base properties of the cation and the anion. If the cation is a weak Lewis acid, it will not affect the pH of the solution. If the cation is the conjugate acid of a weak base or a relatively highly charged metal cation, however, it will react with water to produce an acidic solution.

**B** If the anion is the conjugate base of a strong acid, it will not affect the pH of the solution. If, however, the anion is the conjugate base of a weak acid, the solution will be basic.

**Solution:**

1.  **A** The K<sup>+</sup> cation has a small positive charge (+1) and a relatively large radius (because it is in the fourth row of the periodic table), so it is a very weak Lewis acid.

    **B** The NO<sub>3</sub><sup>−</sup> anion is the conjugate base of a strong acid, so it has essentially no basic character ([Table 16.1 \"Definitions of Acids and Bases\"](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s01-t01)). Hence neither the cation nor the anion will react with water to produce H<sup>+</sup> or OH<sup>−</sup>, and the solution will be neutral.

2.  **A** The Cr<sup>3+</sup> ion is a relatively highly charged metal cation that should behave similarly to the Al<sup>3+</sup> ion and form the \[Cr(H<sub>2</sub>O)<sub>6</sub>\]<sup>3+</sup> complex, which will behave as a weak acid:

    ![Chemical illustration from Chapter 16: Aqueous Acid--Base Equilibriums](/images/section_20/5c666c1597c1833dc689a6022dd8a9bb.jpg)

     

    **B** The Br<sup>−</sup> anion is a very weak base (it is the conjugate base of the strong acid HBr), so it does not affect the pH of the solution. Hence the solution will be acidic.

3.  **A** The Na<sup>+</sup> ion, like the K<sup>+</sup>, is a very weak acid, so it should not affect the acidity of the solution.

    **B** In contrast, SO<sub>4</sub><sup>2−</sup> is the conjugate base of HSO<sub>4</sub><sup>−</sup>, which is a weak acid. Hence the SO<sub>4</sub><sup>2−</sup> ion will react with water as shown in [Figure 16.6](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s04-eq02) to give a slightly basic solution.

Exercise

Predict whether aqueous solutions of the following are acidic, basic, or neutral.

1.  KI
2.  Mg(ClO<sub>4</sub>)<sub>2</sub>
3.  NaHS

**Answer:**

1.  neutral
2.  acidic
3.  basic (due to the reaction of HS<sup>−</sup> with water to form H<sub>2</sub>S and OH<sup>−</sup>)

````````````{important} Summary

Two species that differ by only a proton constitute a **conjugate acid--base pair**. The magnitude of the equilibrium constant for an ionization reaction can be used to determine the relative strengths of acids and bases. For an aqueous solution of a weak acid, the dissociation constant is called the **acid ionization constant (***K*<sub>**a**</sub>**)**. Similarly, the equilibrium constant for the reaction of a weak base with water is the **base ionization constant (***K*<sub>**b**</sub>**)**. For any conjugate acid--base pair, *K*<sub>a</sub>*K*<sub>b</sub> = *K*<sub>w</sub>. Smaller values of p*K*<sub>a</sub> correspond to larger acid ionization constants and hence stronger acids. Conversely, smaller values of p*K*<sub>b</sub> correspond to larger base ionization constants and hence stronger bases. At 25°C, p*K*<sub>a</sub> + p*K*<sub>b</sub> = 14.00. Acid--base reactions always proceed in the direction that produces the weaker acid--base pair. No acid stronger than H<sub>3</sub>O<sup>+</sup> and no base stronger than OH<sup>−</sup> can exist in aqueous solution, leading to the phenomenon known as the **leveling effect**. Polyprotic acids (and bases) lose (and gain) protons in a stepwise manner, with the fully protonated species being the strongest acid and the fully deprotonated species the strongest base. A salt can dissolve in water to produce a neutral, a basic, or an acidic solution, depending on whether it contains the conjugate base of a weak acid as the anion (A<sup>−</sup>), the conjugate acid of a weak base as the cation (BH<sup>+</sup>), or both. Salts that contain small, highly charged metal ions produce acidic solutions in water. The reaction of a salt with water to produce an acidic or a basic solution is called a **hydrolysis reaction**.

````````````

````````````{important} Key Takeaways

- Acid--base reactions always contain two conjugate acid--base pairs.
- Each acid and each base has an associated ionization constant that corresponds to its acid or base strength.

````````````

````````````{important} Key Equations

**Acid ionization constant**

[Equation 16.16](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s02-eq03): $K_{\text{a}} = \frac{\lbrack\text{H}_{3}\text{O}^{+}\rbrack\lbrack\text{A}^{-}\rbrack}{\text{[HA]}}$

**Base ionization constant**

[Equation 16.18](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s02-eq05): $K_{\text{b}} = \frac{\lbrack\text{BH}^{+}\rbrack\lbrack\text{OH}^{-}\rbrack}{\text{[B]}}$

**Relationship between** *K* **<sub>a</sub> and** *K* **<sub>b</sub> of a conjugate acid--base pair**

[Equation 16.23](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s02-eq10): *K*<sub>a</sub>*K*<sub>b</sub> = *K*<sub>w</sub>

**Definition of p** *K* **<sub>a</sub>**

[Equation 16.24](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s02-eq11): p*K*<sub>a</sub> = −log<sub>10</sub>*K*<sub>a</sub>

[Equation 16.25](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s02-eq12): $K_{\text{a}} = \text{1}0^{- \text{p}K_{\text{a}}}$

**Definition of p** *K* **<sub>b</sub>**

[Equation 16.26](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s02-eq13): p*K*<sub>a</sub> = −log<sub>10</sub>*K*<sub>a</sub>

[Equation 16.27](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s02-eq14): $K_{\text{b}} = 10^{- \text{p}K_{\text{b}}}$

**Relationship between p** *K* **<sub>a</sub> and p** *K* **<sub>b</sub> of a conjugate acid--base pair**

[Equation 16.28](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s02-eq15): p*K*<sub>a</sub> + p*K*<sub>b</sub> = p*K*<sub>w</sub>

[Equation 16.29](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s02-eq16): p*K*<sub>a</sub> + p*K*<sub>b</sub> = 14.00 (at 25°C)

````````````

### Conceptual Problems

1.  Identify the conjugate acid--base pairs in each equilibrium.

    1.  $\left. \text{HSO}_{4}{}^{-}\text{(aq)} + \text{H}_{2}\text{O(l)}\rightleftharpoons\text{SO}_{4}{}^{2 -}\text{(aq)} + \text{H}_{3}\text{O}^{+}\text{(aq)} \right.$
    2.  $\left. \text{C}_{3}\text{H}_{7}\text{NO}_{2}\text{(aq)} + \text{H}_{3}\text{O}^{+}\text{(aq)}\rightleftharpoons\text{C}_{3}\text{H}_{8}\text{NO}_{2}{}^{+}\text{(aq)} + \text{H}_{2}\text{O(l)} \right.$
    3.  $\left. \text{CH}_{3}\text{CO}_{2}\text{H(aq)} + \text{NH}_{3}\text{(aq)}\rightleftharpoons\text{CH}_{3}\text{CO}_{2}{}^{-}\text{(aq)} + \text{NH}_{4}{}^{+}\text{(aq)} \right.$
    4.  $\left. \text{SbF}_{5}\text{(aq)} + 2\text{HF(aq)}\rightleftharpoons\text{H}_{2}\text{F}^{+}\text{(aq)} + \text{SbF}_{6}{}^{-}\text{(aq)} \right.$

2.  Identify the conjugate acid--base pairs in each equilibrium.

    1.  $\left. \text{HF(aq)} + \text{H}_{2}\text{O(l)}\rightleftharpoons\text{H}_{3}\text{O}^{+}\text{(aq)} + \text{F}^{-}\text{(aq)} \right.$
    2.  $\left. \text{CH}_{3}\text{CH}_{2}\text{NH}_{2}\text{(aq)} + \text{H}_{2}\text{O(l)}\rightleftharpoons\text{CH}_{3}\text{CH}_{2}\text{NH}_{3}{}^{+}\text{(aq)} + \text{OH}^{-}\text{(aq)} \right.$
    3.  $\left. \text{C}_{3}\text{H}_{7}\text{NO}_{2}\text{(aq)} + \text{OH}^{-}\text{(aq)}\rightleftharpoons\text{C}_{3}\text{H}_{6}\text{NO}_{2}{}^{-}\text{(aq)} + \text{H}_{2}\text{O(l)} \right.$
    4.  $\left. \text{CH}_{3}\text{CO}_{2}\text{H(aq)} + 2\text{HF(aq)}\rightleftharpoons\text{CH}_{3}\text{C(OH)}_{2}{}^{+}\text{(aq)} + \text{HF}_{2}{}^{-}\text{(aq)} \right.$

3.  Salts such as NaH contain the hydride ion (H<sup>−</sup>). When sodium hydride is added to water, it produces hydrogen gas in a highly vigorous reaction. Write a balanced chemical equation for this reaction and identify the conjugate acid--base pairs.

4.  Write the expression for *K*<sub>a</sub> for each reaction.

    1.  $\left. \text{HCO}_{3}{}^{-}\text{(aq)} + \text{H}_{2}\text{O(l)}\rightleftharpoons\text{CO}_{3}{}^{2 -}\text{(aq)} + \text{H}_{3}\text{O}^{+}\text{(aq)} \right.$
    2.  $\left. \text{formic~acid(aq)} + \text{H}_{2}\text{O(l)}\rightleftharpoons\text{formate(aq)} + \text{H}_{3}\text{O}^{+}\text{(aq)} \right.$
    3.  $\left. \text{H}_{3}\text{PO}_{4}\text{(aq)} + \text{H}_{2}\text{O(l)}\rightleftharpoons\text{H}_{2}\text{PO}_{4}{}^{-}\text{(aq)} + \text{H}_{3}\text{O}^{+}\text{(aq)} \right.$

5.  Write an expression for the ionization constant *K*<sub>b</sub> for each reaction.

    1.  $\left. \text{OCH}_{3}{}^{-}\text{(aq)} + \text{H}_{2}\text{O(l)}\rightleftharpoons\text{HOCH}_{3}\text{(aq)} + \text{OH}^{-}\text{(aq)} \right.$
    2.  $\left. \text{NH}_{2}{}^{-}\text{(aq)} + \text{H}_{2}\text{O(l)}\rightleftharpoons\text{NH}_{3}\text{(aq)} + \text{OH}^{-}\text{(aq)} \right.$
    3.  $\left. \text{S}^{2 -}\text{(aq)} + \text{H}_{2}\text{O(l)}\rightleftharpoons\text{HS}^{-}\text{(aq)} + \text{OH}^{-}\text{(aq)} \right.$

6.  Predict whether each equilibrium lies primarily to the left or to the right.

    1.  $\left. \text{HBr(aq)} + \text{H}_{2}\text{O(l)}\rightleftharpoons\text{H}_{3}\text{O}^{+}\text{(aq)} + \text{Br}^{-}\text{(aq)} \right.$
    2.  $\left. \text{NaH(soln)} + \text{NH}_{3}\text{(l)}\rightleftharpoons\text{H}_{2}\text{(soln)} + \text{NaNH}_{2}\text{(soln)} \right.$
    3.  $\left. \text{OCH}_{3}{}^{-}\text{(aq)} + \text{NH}_{3}\text{(aq)}\rightleftharpoons\text{CH}_{3}\text{OH(aq)} + \text{NH}_{2}{}^{-}\text{(aq)} \right.$
    4.  $\left. \text{NH}_{3}\text{(aq)} + \text{HCl(aq)}\rightleftharpoons\text{NH}_{4}{}^{+}\text{(aq)} + \text{Cl}^{-}\text{(aq)} \right.$

7.  Species that are strong bases in water, such as CH<sub>3</sub><sup>−</sup>, NH<sub>2</sub><sup>−</sup>, and S<sup>2−</sup>, are leveled to the strength of OH<sup>−</sup>, the conjugate base of H<sub>2</sub>O. Because their relative base strengths are indistinguishable in water, suggest a method for identifying which is the strongest base. How would you distinguish between the strength of the acids HIO<sub>3</sub>, H<sub>2</sub>SO<sub>4</sub>, and HClO<sub>4</sub>?

8.  Is it accurate to say that a 2.0 M solution of H<sub>2</sub>SO<sub>4</sub>, which contains two acidic protons per molecule, is 4.0 M in H<sup>+</sup>? Explain your answer.

9.  The alkalinity of soil is defined by the following equation: alkalinity = \[HCO<sub>3</sub><sup>−</sup>\] + 2\[CO<sub>3</sub><sup>2−</sup>\] + \[OH<sup>−</sup>\] − \[H<sup>+</sup>\]. The source of both HCO<sub>3</sub><sup>−</sup> and CO<sub>3</sub><sup>2−</sup> is H<sub>2</sub>CO<sub>3</sub>. Explain why the basicity of soil is defined in this way.

10. Why are aqueous solutions of salts such as CaCl<sub>2</sub> neutral? Why is an aqueous solution of NaNH<sub>2</sub> basic?

11. Predict whether aqueous solutions of the following are acidic, basic, or neutral.

    1.  Li<sub>3</sub>N
    2.  NaH
    3.  KBr
    4.  C<sub>2</sub>H<sub>5</sub>NH<sub>3</sub><sup>+</sup>Cl<sup>−</sup>

12. When each compound is added to water, would you expect the pH of the solution to increase, decrease, or remain the same?

    1.  LiCH<sub>3</sub>
    2.  MgCl<sub>2</sub>
    3.  K<sub>2</sub>O
    4.  (CH<sub>3</sub>)<sub>2</sub>NH<sub>2</sub><sup>+</sup>Br<sup>−</sup>

13. Which complex ion would you expect to be more acidic---Pb(H<sub>2</sub>O)<sub>4</sub><sup>2+</sup> or Sn(H<sub>2</sub>O)<sub>4</sub><sup>2+</sup>? Why?

14. Would you expect Sn(H<sub>2</sub>O)<sub>4</sub><sup>2+</sup> or Sn(H<sub>2</sub>O)<sub>6</sub><sup>4+</sup> to be more acidic? Why?

15. Is it possible to arrange the hydrides LiH, RbH, KH, CsH, and NaH in order of increasing base strength in aqueous solution? Why or why not?

### Answer

1.  1.  $\left. \overset{\text{acid~}1}{\text{HSO}_{4}{}^{-}\text{(aq)~}} + \underset{\text{base~}2}{\text{H}_{2}\text{O(l)}}\rightleftharpoons\overset{\text{base~}1}{\text{SO}_{4}{}^{2 -}\text{(aq)}}\text{~+~}\underset{\text{acid~}2}{\text{H}_{3}\text{O}^{+}\text{(aq)}} \right.$

### Numerical Problems

1.  Arrange these acids in order of increasing strength.

    - acid A: p*K*<sub>a</sub> = 1.52
    - acid B: p*K*<sub>a</sub> = 6.93
    - acid C: p*K*<sub>a</sub> = 3.86

    Given solutions with the same initial concentration of each acid, which would have the highest percent ionization?

(src-averill-1-0-ch16-s03)=

## 16.3 Molecular Structure and Acid--Base Strength

````````````{note} Learning Objective

1.  To understand how molecular structure affects the strength of an acid or base.

````````````

We have seen that the strengths of acids and bases vary over many orders of magnitude. In this section, we explore some of the structural and electronic factors that control the acidity or basicity of a molecule.

### Bond Strengths

In general, the stronger the A--H or B--H<sup>+</sup> bond, the less likely the bond is to break to form H<sup>+</sup> ions and thus the less acidic the substance. This effect can be illustrated using the hydrogen halides:

````````{list-table}
:header-rows: 1

- - Relative Acid Strength
  - HF
  - \<
  - HCl
  - \<
  - HBr
  - \<
  - HI
- - H--X Bond Energy (kJ/mol)
  - 570
  -  
  - 432
  -  
  - 366
  -  
  - 298
- - p*K*<sub>a</sub>
  - 3.20
  -  
  - −6.1
  -  
  - −8.9
  -  
  - −9.3

````````

The trend in bond energies is due to a steady decrease in overlap between the 1*s* orbital of hydrogen and the valence orbital of the halogen atom as the size of the halogen increases. The larger the atom to which H is bonded, the weaker the bond. Thus the bond between H and a large atom in a given family, such as I or Te, is weaker than the bond between H and a smaller atom in the same family, such as F or O. As a result, *acid strengths of binary hydrides increase as we go down a column of the periodic table*. For example, the order of acidity for the binary hydrides of Group 16 is as follows, with p*K*<sub>a</sub> values in parentheses: H<sub>2</sub>O (14.00 = p*K*<sub>w</sub>) \< H<sub>2</sub>S (7.05) \< H<sub>2</sub>Se (3.89) \< H<sub>2</sub>Te (2.6).

### Stability of the Conjugate Base

Whether we write an acid--base reaction as $\left. \text{AH}\rightleftharpoons\text{A}^{-} + \text{H}^{+} \right.$ or as $\left. \text{BH}^{+}\rightleftharpoons\text{B} + \text{H}^{+}\text{,} \right.$ the conjugate base (A<sup>−</sup> or B) contains one more lone pair of electrons than the parent acid (AH or BH<sup>+</sup>). *Any factor that stabilizes the lone pair on the conjugate base favors dissociation of H+ and makes the parent acid a stronger acid.* Let's see how this explains the relative acidity of the binary hydrides of the elements in the second row of the periodic table. The observed order of increasing acidity is the following, with p*K*<sub>a</sub> values in parentheses: CH<sub>4</sub> (~50) \<\< NH<sub>3</sub> (~36) \< H<sub>2</sub>O (14.00) \< HF (3.20). Consider, for example, the compounds at both ends of this series: methane and hydrogen fluoride. The conjugate base of CH<sub>4</sub> is CH<sub>3</sub><sup>−</sup>, and the conjugate base of HF is F<sup>−</sup>. Because fluorine is much more electronegative than carbon, fluorine can better stabilize the negative charge in the F<sup>−</sup> ion than carbon can stabilize the negative charge in the CH<sub>3</sub><sup>−</sup> ion. Consequently, HF has a greater tendency to dissociate to form H<sup>+</sup> and F<sup>−</sup> than does methane to form H<sup>+</sup> and CH<sub>3</sub><sup>−</sup>, making HF a much stronger acid than CH<sub>4</sub>.

The same trend is predicted by analyzing the properties of the conjugate acids. For a series of compounds of the general formula HE, as the electronegativity of E increases, the E--H bond becomes more polar, favoring dissociation to form E<sup>−</sup> and H<sup>+</sup>. Due to both the increasing stability of the conjugate base and the increasing polarization of the E--H bond in the conjugate acid, *acid strengths of binary hydrides increase as we go from left to right across a row of the periodic table*.

````````````{important} Note the Pattern

Acid strengths of binary hydrides *increase* as we go *down* a column or *from left to right* across a row of the periodic table.

````````````

### Inductive Effects

Atoms or groups of atoms in a molecule other than those to which H is bonded can induce a change in the distribution of electrons within the molecule. This is called an *inductive effect*, and, much like the coordination of water to a metal ion, it can have a major effect on the acidity or basicity of the molecule. For example, the hypohalous acids (general formula HOX, with X representing a halogen) all have a hydrogen atom bonded to an oxygen atom. In aqueous solution, they all produce the following equilibrium:

Equation 16.37

$$\left. \text{HOX(aq)}\rightleftharpoons\text{H}^{+}\text{(aq)} + \text{OX}^{-}\text{(aq)} \right.$$

The acidities of these acids vary by about three orders of magnitude, however, due to the difference in electronegativity of the halogen atoms:

````````{list-table}
:header-rows: 1

- - HOX
  - Electronegativity of X
  - p*K*<sub>a</sub>
- - HOCl
  - 3.0
  - 7.40
- - HOBr
  - 2.8
  - 8.55
- - HOI
  - 2.5
  - 10.5

````````

As the electronegativity of X increases, the distribution of electron density within the molecule changes: the electrons are drawn more strongly toward the halogen atom and, in turn, away from the H in the O--H bond, thus weakening the O--H bond and allowing dissociation of hydrogen as H<sup>+</sup>.

The acidity of oxoacids, with the general formula HOXO<sub>*n*</sub> (*n* = 0−3), depends strongly on the number of terminal oxygen atoms attached to the central atom X. As shown in [Figure 16.14 \"The Relationship between the Acid Strengths of the Oxoacids of Chlorine and the Electron Density on the O--H Unit\"](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s03-s03-f01), the *K*<sub>a</sub> values of the oxoacids of chlorine increase by a factor of about 10<sup>4</sup> to 10<sup>6</sup> with each oxygen as successive oxygen atoms are added. The increase in acid strength with increasing number of terminal oxygen atoms is due to both an inductive effect and increased stabilization of the conjugate base.

````````````{important} Note the Pattern

Any inductive effect that withdraws electron density from an O--H bond increases the acidity of the compound.

````````````

Because oxygen is the second most electronegative element, adding terminal oxygen atoms causes electrons to be drawn away from the O--H bond, making it weaker and thereby increasing the strength of the acid. The colors in [Figure 16.14 \"The Relationship between the Acid Strengths of the Oxoacids of Chlorine and the Electron Density on the O--H Unit\"](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s03-s03-f01) show how the electrostatic potential, a measure of the strength of the interaction of a point charge at any place on the surface of the molecule, changes as the number of terminal oxygen atoms increases. In [Figure 16.14 \"The Relationship between the Acid Strengths of the Oxoacids of Chlorine and the Electron Density on the O--H Unit\"](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s03-s03-f01) and [Figure 16.15 \"The Relationship between Delocalization of the Negative Charge in the Oxoanions of Chlorine and the Number of Terminal Oxygen Atoms\"](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s03-s03-f02), blue corresponds to low electron densities, while red corresponds to high electron densities. The oxygen atom in the O--H unit becomes steadily less red from HClO to HClO<sub>4</sub> (also written as HOClO<sub>3</sub>), while the H atom becomes steadily bluer, indicating that the electron density on the O--H unit decreases as the number of terminal oxygen atoms increases. The decrease in electron density in the O--H bond weakens it, making it easier to lose hydrogen as H<sup>+</sup> ions, thereby increasing the strength of the acid.

(src-averill-1-0-ch16-s03-s03-f01)=

Figure 16.14 The Relationship between the Acid Strengths of the Oxoacids of Chlorine and the Electron Density on the O--H Unit

![Figure 16.14 The Relationship between the Acid Strengths of the Oxoacids of Chlorine and the Electron Density on the O--H Unit](/images/section_20/cbfd507c1a19b98db1f5201e5ab728ed.jpg)

These electrostatic potential maps show how the electron density on the O--H unit decreases as the number of terminal oxygen atoms increases. Blue corresponds to low electron densities, whereas red corresponds to high electron densities.

Source: Chlorine oxoacids p*K*<sub>a</sub> values from J. R. Bowser, *Inorganic Chemistry* (Pacific Grove, CA: Brooks-Cole,1993).

At least as important, however, is the effect of delocalization of the negative charge in the conjugate base. As shown in [Figure 16.15 \"The Relationship between Delocalization of the Negative Charge in the Oxoanions of Chlorine and the Number of Terminal Oxygen Atoms\"](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s03-s03-f02), the number of resonance structures that can be written for the oxoanions of chlorine increases as the number of terminal oxygen atoms increases, allowing the single negative charge to be delocalized over successively more oxygen atoms. The electrostatic potential plots in [Figure 16.15 \"The Relationship between Delocalization of the Negative Charge in the Oxoanions of Chlorine and the Number of Terminal Oxygen Atoms\"](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s03-s03-f02) demonstrate that the electron density on the terminal oxygen atoms decreases steadily as their number increases. The oxygen atom in ClO<sup>−</sup> is red, indicating that it is electron rich, and the color of oxygen progressively changes to green in ClO<sub>4</sub><sup>−</sup>, indicating that the oxygen atoms are becoming steadily less electron rich through the series. For example, in the perchlorate ion (ClO<sub>4</sub><sup>−</sup>), the single negative charge is delocalized over all four oxygen atoms, whereas in the hypochlorite ion (OCl<sup>−</sup>), the negative charge is largely localized on a single oxygen atom ([Figure 16.15 \"The Relationship between Delocalization of the Negative Charge in the Oxoanions of Chlorine and the Number of Terminal Oxygen Atoms\"](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s03-s03-f02)). As a result, the perchlorate ion has no localized negative charge to which a proton can bind. Consequently, the perchlorate anion has a much lower affinity for a proton than does the hypochlorite ion, and perchloric acid is one of the strongest acids known.

````````````{important} Note the Pattern

Electron delocalization in the conjugate base increases acid strength.

````````````

(src-averill-1-0-ch16-s03-s03-f02)=

Figure 16.15 The Relationship between Delocalization of the Negative Charge in the Oxoanions of Chlorine and the Number of Terminal Oxygen Atoms

![Figure 16.15 The Relationship between Delocalization of the Negative Charge in the Oxoanions of Chlorine and the Number of Terminal Oxygen Atoms](/images/section_20/99db2ab846ba9253de6cfe75d12abf9c.jpg)

As the number of terminal oxygen atoms increases, the number of resonance structures that can be written for the oxoanions of chlorine also increases, and the single negative charge is delocalized over more oxygen atoms. As these electrostatic potential plots demonstrate, the electron density on the terminal oxygen atoms decreases steadily as their number increases. As the electron density on the oxygen atoms decreases, so does their affinity for a proton, making the anion less basic. As a result, the parent oxoacid is more acidic.

Similar inductive effects are also responsible for the trend in the acidities of oxoacids that have the same number of oxygen atoms as we go across a row of the periodic table from left to right. For example, H<sub>3</sub>PO<sub>4</sub> is a weak acid, H<sub>2</sub>SO<sub>4</sub> is a strong acid, and HClO<sub>4</sub> is one of the strongest acids known. The number of terminal oxygen atoms increases steadily across the row, consistent with the observed increase in acidity. In addition, the electronegativity of the central atom increases steadily from P to S to Cl, which causes electrons to be drawn from oxygen to the central atom, weakening the O--H bond and increasing the strength of the oxoacid.

Careful inspection of the data in [Table 16.4 \"Values of p\"](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s03-s03-t01) shows two apparent anomalies: carbonic acid and phosphorous acid. If carbonic acid (H<sub>2</sub>CO<sub>3</sub>) were a discrete molecule with the structure (HO)<sub>2</sub>C=O, it would have a single terminal oxygen atom and should be comparable in acid strength to phosphoric acid (H<sub>3</sub>PO<sub>4</sub>), for which p*K*<sub>a1</sub> = 2.16. Instead, the tabulated value of p*K*<sub>a1</sub> for carbonic acid is 6.35, making it about 10,000 times weaker than expected. As we shall see in [Section 16.6 \"Buffers\"](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s06), however, H<sub>2</sub>CO<sub>3</sub> is only a minor component of the aqueous solutions of CO<sub>2</sub> that are referred to as carbonic acid. Similarly, if phosphorous acid (H<sub>3</sub>PO<sub>3</sub>) actually had the structure (HO)<sub>3</sub>P, it would have no terminal oxygen atoms attached to phosphorous. It would therefore be expected to be about as strong an acid as HOCl (p*K*<sub>a</sub> = 7.40). In fact, the p*K*<sub>a1</sub> for phosphorous acid is 1.30, and the structure of phosphorous acid is (HO)<sub>2</sub>P(=O)H with one H atom directly bonded to P and one P=O bond. Thus the p*K*<sub>a1</sub> for phosphorous acid is similar to that of other oxoacids with one terminal oxygen atom, such as H<sub>3</sub>PO<sub>4</sub>. Fortunately, phosphorous acid is the only common oxoacid in which a hydrogen atom is bonded to the central atom rather than oxygen.

![Chemical illustration from Chapter 16: Aqueous Acid--Base Equilibriums](/images/section_20/e6b38ba319c5c580e8fb67a7fe94b286.jpg)
