Inductive effects are also observed in organic molecules that contain electronegative substituents. The magnitude of the electron-withdrawing effect depends on both the nature and the number of halogen substituents, as shown by the p*K*<sub>a</sub> values for several acetic acid derivatives:

$$\underset{\text{p}K_{\text{a}}}{\ }\underset{4.76}{\text{CH}_{\text{3}}\text{CO}_{\text{2}}\text{H}} < \underset{2.87}{\text{CH}_{\text{2}}\text{ClCO}_{\text{2}}\text{H}} < \underset{1.35}{\text{CHCl}_{\text{2}}\text{CO}_{\text{2}}\text{H}} < \underset{0.66}{\text{CCl}_{\text{3}}\text{CO}_{\text{2}}\text{H}} < \underset{0.52}{\text{CF}_{\text{3}}\text{CO}_{\text{2}}\text{H}}$$

As you might expect, fluorine, which is more electronegative than chlorine, causes a larger effect than chlorine, and the effect of three halogens is greater than the effect of two or one. Notice from these data that inductive effects can be quite large. For instance, replacing the --CH<sub>3</sub> group of acetic acid by a --CF<sub>3</sub> group results in about a 10,000-fold increase in acidity!

### Example 5

Arrange the compounds of each series in order of increasing acid or base strength.

1.  sulfuric acid \[H<sub>2</sub>SO<sub>4</sub>, or (HO)<sub>2</sub>SO<sub>2</sub>\], fluorosulfonic acid (FSO<sub>3</sub>H, or FSO<sub>2</sub>OH), and sulfurous acid \[H<sub>2</sub>SO<sub>3</sub>, or (HO)<sub>2</sub>SO\]
2.  ammonia (NH<sub>3</sub>), trifluoramine (NF<sub>3</sub>), and hydroxylamine (NH<sub>2</sub>OH)

The structures are shown here.

![Chemical illustration from Chapter 16: Aqueous Acid--Base Equilibriums](/images/section_20/f30afdb9ff3120fa608040e4a462ff3b.jpg)

**Given:** series of compounds

**Asked for:** relative acid or base strengths

**Strategy:**

Use relative bond strengths, the stability of the conjugate base, and inductive effects to arrange the compounds in order of increasing tendency to ionize in aqueous solution.

**Solution:**

1.  Although both sulfuric acid and sulfurous acid have two --OH groups, the sulfur atom in sulfuric acid is bonded to two terminal oxygen atoms versus one in sulfurous acid. Because oxygen is highly electronegative, sulfuric acid is the stronger acid because the negative charge on the anion is stabilized by the additional oxygen atom. In comparing sulfuric acid and fluorosulfonic acid, we note that fluorine is more electronegative than oxygen. Thus replacing an --OH by --F will remove *more* electron density from the central S atom, which will, in turn, remove electron density from the S--OH bond and the O--H bond. Because its O--H bond is weaker, FSO<sub>3</sub>H is a stronger acid than sulfuric acid. The predicted order of acid strengths given here is confirmed by the measured p*K*<sub>a</sub> values for these acids:

    $$\underset{\text{p}K_{\text{a}}}{\,}\underset{1.85}{\text{H}_{2}\text{SO}_{3}} < \underset{- 2}{\text{H}_{2}\text{SO}_{4}} < \underset{- 10}{\text{FSO}_{3}\text{H}}$$

2.  The structures of both trifluoramine and hydroxylamine are similar to that of ammonia. In trifluoramine, all of the hydrogen atoms in NH<sub>3</sub> are replaced by fluorine atoms, whereas in hydroxylamine, one hydrogen atom is replaced by OH. Replacing the three hydrogen atoms by fluorine will withdraw electron density from N, making the lone electron pair on N less available to bond to an H<sup>+</sup> ion. Thus NF<sub>3</sub> is predicted to be a much weaker base than NH<sub>3</sub>. Similarly, because oxygen is more electronegative than hydrogen, replacing one hydrogen atom in NH<sub>3</sub> by OH will make the amine less basic. Because oxygen is less electronegative than fluorine and only one hydrogen atom is replaced, however, the effect will be smaller. The predicted order of increasing base strength shown here is confirmed by the measured p*K*<sub>b</sub> values:

    $$\underset{\text{p}K_{\text{b}}}{\,\ }\underset{-}{\text{NF}_{3}} < < \underset{8.06}{\text{NH}_{2}\text{OH}} < \underset{4.75}{\text{NH}_{3}}$$

    Trifluoramine is such a weak base that it does not react with aqueous solutions of strong acids. Hence its base ionization constant has not been measured.

Exercise

Arrange the compounds of each series in order of

1.  decreasing acid strength: H<sub>3</sub>PO<sub>4</sub>, CH<sub>3</sub>PO<sub>3</sub>H<sub>2</sub>, and HClO<sub>3</sub>.
2.  increasing base strength: CH<sub>3</sub>S<sup>−</sup>, OH<sup>−</sup>, and CF<sub>3</sub>S<sup>−</sup>.

**Answer:**

1.  HClO<sub>3</sub> \> CH<sub>3</sub>PO<sub>3</sub>H<sub>2</sub> \> H<sub>3</sub>PO<sub>4</sub>
2.  CF<sub>3</sub>S<sup>−</sup> \< CH<sub>3</sub>S<sup>−</sup> \< OH<sup>−</sup>

````````````{important} Summary

The acid--base strength of a molecule depends strongly on its structure. The weaker the A--H or B--H<sup>+</sup> bond, the more likely it is to dissociate to form an H<sup>+</sup> ion. In addition, any factor that stabilizes the lone pair on the conjugate base favors the dissociation of H<sup>+</sup>, making the conjugate acid a stronger acid. Atoms or groups of atoms elsewhere in a molecule can also be important in determining acid or base strength through an *inductive effect*, which can weaken an O--H bond and allow hydrogen to be more easily lost as H<sup>+</sup> ions.

````````````

````````````{important} Key Takeaway

- Inductive effects and charge delocalization significantly influence the acidity or basicity of a compound.

````````````

### Conceptual Problems

1.  [Section 16.3 \"Molecular Structure and Acid--Base Strength\"](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s03) presented several factors that affect the relative strengths of acids and bases. For each pair, identify the most important factor in determining which is the stronger acid or base in aqueous solution.

    1.  CH<sub>3</sub>CCl<sub>2</sub>CH<sub>2</sub>CO<sub>2</sub>H versus CH<sub>3</sub>CH<sub>2</sub>CH<sub>2</sub>CO<sub>2</sub>H
    2.  CH<sub>3</sub>CO<sub>2</sub>H versus CH<sub>3</sub>CH<sub>2</sub>OH
    3.  HClO versus HBrO
    4.  CH<sub>3</sub>C(=O)NH<sub>2</sub> versus CH<sub>3</sub>CH<sub>2</sub>NH<sub>2</sub>
    5.  H<sub>3</sub>AsO<sub>4</sub> versus H<sub>3</sub>AsO<sub>3</sub>

2.  The stability of the conjugate base is an important factor in determining the strength of an acid. Which would you expect to be the stronger acid in aqueous solution---C<sub>6</sub>H<sub>5</sub>NH<sub>3</sub><sup>+</sup> or NH<sub>4</sub><sup>+</sup>? Justify your reasoning.

3.  Explain why H<sub>2</sub>Se is a weaker acid than HBr.

4.  Arrange the following in order of decreasing acid strength in aqueous solution: H<sub>3</sub>PO<sub>4</sub>, CH<sub>3</sub>PO<sub>3</sub>H<sub>2</sub>, and HClO<sub>3</sub>.

5.  Arrange the following in order of increasing base strength in aqueous solution: CH<sub>3</sub>S<sup>−</sup>, OH<sup>−</sup>, and CF<sub>3</sub>S<sup>−</sup>.

6.  Arrange the following in order of increasing acid strength in aqueous solution: HClO<sub>2</sub>, HNO<sub>2</sub>, and HNO<sub>3</sub>.

7.  Do you expect H<sub>2</sub>SO<sub>3</sub> or H<sub>2</sub>SeO<sub>3</sub> to be the stronger acid? Why?

8.  Give a plausible explanation for why CF<sub>3</sub>OH is a stronger acid than CH<sub>3</sub>OH in aqueous solution. Do you expect CHCl<sub>2</sub>CH<sub>2</sub>OH to be a stronger or a weaker acid than CH<sub>3</sub>OH? Why?

9.  Do you expect Cl<sub>2</sub>NH or NH<sub>3</sub> to be the stronger base in aqueous solution? Why?

### Answers

1.  

2.  

3.  

4.  

5.  CF<sub>3</sub>S<sup>−</sup> \< CH<sub>3</sub>S<sup>−</sup> \< OH<sup>−</sup> (strongest base)

6.  

7.  

8.  

9.  NH<sub>3</sub>; Cl atoms withdraw electron density from N in Cl<sub>2</sub>NH.

(src-averill-1-0-ch16-s04)=

## 16.4 Quantitative Aspects of Acid--Base Equilibriums

````````````{note} Learning Objective

1.  To use *K*<sub>a</sub> and *K*<sub>b</sub> values to calculate the percent ionization and the pH of a solution of an acid or a base.

````````````

This section presents a quantitative approach to analyzing acid--base equilibriums. You will learn how to determine the values of *K*<sub>a</sub> and *K*<sub>b</sub>, how to use *K*<sub>a</sub> or *K*<sub>b</sub> to calculate the percent ionization and the pH of an aqueous solution of an acid or a base, and how to calculate the equilibrium constant for the reaction of an acid with a base from the *K*<sub>a</sub> and *K*<sub>b</sub> of the reactants.

### Determining *K*<sub>a</sub> and *K*<sub>b</sub>

The ionization constants *K*<sub>a</sub> and *K*<sub>b</sub> are equilibrium constants that are calculated from experimentally measured concentrations, just like the equilibrium constants discussed in [Chapter 15 \"Chemical Equilibrium\"](/chapters/ch-15-chemical-equilibrium.md#src-averill-1-0-ch15). Before proceeding further, it is important to understand exactly what is meant when we describe the concentration of an aqueous solution of a weak acid or a weak base. Suppose, for example, we have a bottle labeled 1.0 M acetic acid or 1.0 M ammonia. As you learned in [Chapter 4 \"Reactions in Aqueous Solution\"](/chapters/ch-04-reactions-in-aqueous-solution.md#src-averill-1-0-ch04), such a solution is usually prepared by dissolving 1.0 mol of acetic acid or ammonia in water and adding enough water to give a final volume of exactly 1.0 L. If, however, we were to list the actual concentrations of all the species present in either solution, we would find that *none* of the values is exactly 1.0 M because a weak acid such as acetic acid or a weak base such as ammonia always reacts with water to some extent. The extent of the reaction depends on the *K*<sub>a</sub> or the *K*<sub>b</sub>, the concentration of the acid or the base, and the temperature. Consequently, only the *total concentration* of both the ionized and unionized species is equal to 1.0 M.

The *analytical concentration* (*C*) is defined as the *total* concentration of all forms of an acid or a base that are present in solution, regardless of their state of protonation. Thus a "1.0 M" solution of acetic acid has an analytical concentration of 1.0 M, which is the sum of the *actual* concentrations of unionized acetic acid (CH<sub>3</sub>CO<sub>2</sub>H) and the ionized form (CH<sub>3</sub>CO<sub>2</sub><sup>−</sup>):

Equation 16.38

$$C_{\text{CH}_{3}\text{CO}_{2}\text{H}} = \lbrack\text{CH}_{3}\text{CO}_{2}\text{H}\rbrack + \lbrack\text{CH}_{3}\text{CO}_{2}{}^{-}\rbrack$$

As we shall see shortly, if we know the analytical concentration and the *K*<sub>a</sub>, we can calculate the actual values of \[CH<sub>3</sub>CO<sub>2</sub>H\] and \[CH<sub>3</sub>CO<sub>2</sub><sup>−</sup>\].

The equilibrium equations for the reaction of acetic acid and ammonia with water are as follows:

Equation 16.39

$$K_{\text{a}} = \frac{\lbrack\text{H}^{+}\rbrack\lbrack\text{CH}_{3}\text{CO}_{2}{}^{-}\rbrack}{\lbrack\text{CH}_{3}\text{CO}_{2}\text{H}\rbrack}$$

Equation 16.40

$$K_{\text{b}} = \frac{\lbrack\text{NH}_{4}{}^{+}\rbrack\lbrack\text{OH}^{-}\rbrack}{\lbrack\text{NH}_{3}\rbrack}$$

where *K*<sub>a</sub> and *K*<sub>b</sub> are the ionization constants for acetic acid and ammonia, respectively. In addition to the analytical concentration of the acid (or the base), we must have a way to measure the concentration of at least *one* of the species in the equilibrium constant expression to determine the *K*<sub>a</sub> (or the *K*<sub>b</sub>). There are two common ways to obtain the concentrations: (1) measure the electrical conductivity of the solution, which is related to the total concentration of ions present, and (2) measure the pH of the solution, which gives \[H<sup>+</sup>\] or \[OH<sup>−</sup>\].

Example 6 and Example 7 illustrate the procedure for determining *K*<sub>a</sub> for a weak acid and *K*<sub>b</sub> for a weak base. In both cases, we will follow the procedure developed in [Chapter 15 \"Chemical Equilibrium\"](/chapters/ch-15-chemical-equilibrium.md#src-averill-1-0-ch15): the analytical concentration of the acid or the base is the *initial* concentration, and the stoichiometry of the reaction with water determines the *change* in concentrations. The *final* concentrations of all species are calculated from the initial concentrations and the changes in the concentrations. Inserting the final concentrations into the equilibrium constant expression enables us to calculate the *K*<sub>a</sub> or the *K*<sub>b</sub>.

### Example 6

Electrical conductivity measurements indicate that 0.42% of the acetic acid molecules in a 1.00 M solution are ionized at 25°C. Calculate *K*<sub>a</sub> and p*K*<sub>a</sub> for acetic acid at this temperature.

**Given:** analytical concentration and percent ionization

**Asked for:** *K*<sub>a</sub> and p*K*<sub>a</sub>

**Strategy:**

**A** Write the balanced equilibrium equation for the reaction and derive the equilibrium constant expression.

**B** Use the data given and the stoichiometry of the reaction to construct a table showing the initial concentrations, the changes in concentrations, and the final concentrations for all species in the equilibrium constant expression.

**C** Substitute the final concentrations into the equilibrium constant expression and calculate the *K*<sub>a</sub>. Take the negative logarithm of *K*<sub>a</sub> to obtain the p*K*<sub>a</sub>.

**Solution:**

**A** The balanced equilibrium equation for the dissociation of acetic acid is as follows:

$$\left. \text{CH}_{3}\text{CO}_{2}\text{H(aq)}\rightleftharpoons\text{H}^{+}\text{(aq)} + \text{CH}_{3}\text{CO}_{2}{}^{-}\text{(aq)} \right.$$

and the equilibrium constant expression is as follows:

$$K_{\text{a}} = \frac{\lbrack\text{H}^{+}\rbrack\lbrack\text{CH}_{3}\text{CO}_{2}{}^{-}\rbrack}{\lbrack\text{CH}_{3}\text{CO}_{2}\text{H}\rbrack}$$

**B** To calculate the *K*<sub>a</sub>, we need to know the equilibrium concentrations of CH<sub>3</sub>CO<sub>2</sub>H, CH<sub>3</sub>CO<sub>2</sub><sup>−</sup>, and H<sup>+</sup>. The most direct way to do this is to construct a table that lists the initial concentrations and the changes in concentrations that occur during the reaction to give the final concentrations, using the procedure introduced in [Chapter 15 \"Chemical Equilibrium\"](/chapters/ch-15-chemical-equilibrium.md#src-averill-1-0-ch15). The initial concentration of unionized acetic acid (\[CH<sub>3</sub>CO<sub>2</sub>H\]<sub>i</sub>) is the analytical concentration, 1.00 M, and the initial acetate concentration (\[CH<sub>3</sub>CO<sub>2</sub><sup>−</sup>\]<sub>i</sub>) is zero. The initial concentration of H<sup>+</sup> is not zero, however; \[H<sup>+</sup>\]<sub>i</sub> is 1.00 × 10<sup>−7</sup> M due to the autoionization of water. The measured percent ionization tells us that 0.42% of the acetic acid molecules are ionized at equilibrium. Consequently, the change in the concentration of acetic acid is Δ\[CH<sub>3</sub>CO<sub>2</sub>H\] = −(4.2 × 10<sup>−3</sup>)(1.00 M) = −0.0042 M. Conversely, the change in the acetate concentration is Δ\[CH<sub>3</sub>CO<sub>2</sub><sup>−</sup>\] = +0.0042 M because every 1 mol of acetic acid that ionizes gives 1 mol of acetate. Because one proton is produced for each acetate ion formed, Δ\[H<sup>+</sup>\] = +0.0042 M as well. These results are summarized in the following table.

````````{list-table}
:header-rows: 2

- - $\left. \text{CH}_{3}\text{CO}_{2}\text{H}\text{(aq)}\rightleftharpoons\text{H}^{+}\text{(aq)} + \text{CH}_{3}\text{CO}_{2}{}^{-}\text{(aq)} \right.$
  - $\left. \text{CH}_{3}\text{CO}_{2}\text{H}\text{(aq)}\rightleftharpoons\text{H}^{+}\text{(aq)} + \text{CH}_{3}\text{CO}_{2}{}^{-}\text{(aq)} \right.$
  - $\left. \text{CH}_{3}\text{CO}_{2}\text{H}\text{(aq)}\rightleftharpoons\text{H}^{+}\text{(aq)} + \text{CH}_{3}\text{CO}_{2}{}^{-}\text{(aq)} \right.$
  - $\left. \text{CH}_{3}\text{CO}_{2}\text{H}\text{(aq)}\rightleftharpoons\text{H}^{+}\text{(aq)} + \text{CH}_{3}\text{CO}_{2}{}^{-}\text{(aq)} \right.$
- -  
  - \[CH<sub>3</sub>CO<sub>2</sub>H\]
  - \[H<sup>+</sup>\]
  - \[CH<sub>3</sub>CO<sub>2</sub><sup>−</sup>\]
- - initial
  - 1.00
  - 1.00 × 10<sup>−7</sup>
  - 0
- - change
  - −0.0042
  - +0.0042
  - +0.0042
- - final
  -  
  -  
  -  

````````

The final concentrations of all species are therefore as follows:

$$\begin{array}{l}
{{\lbrack\text{CH}_{3}\text{CO}_{2}\text{H}\rbrack}_{\text{f}} = {\lbrack\text{CH}_{3}\text{CO}_{2}\text{H}\rbrack}_{\text{i}} + \Delta\lbrack\text{CH}_{3}\text{CO}_{2}\text{H}\rbrack = 1.00\text{~M} + ( - 0.0042\text{~M}) = 1.00\text{~M}} \\
{{\lbrack\text{CH}_{3}\text{CO}_{2}{}^{-}\rbrack}_{\text{f}} = {\lbrack\text{CH}_{3}\text{CO}_{2}{}^{-}\rbrack}_{\text{i}} + \Delta\lbrack\text{CH}_{3}\text{CO}_{2}{}^{-}\rbrack = 0\text{~M} + ( + 0.0042\text{~M}) = 0.0042\text{~M}} \\
{{\lbrack\text{H}^{+}\rbrack}_{\text{f}} = {\lbrack\text{H}^{+}\rbrack}_{\text{i}} + \Delta\lbrack\text{H}^{+}\rbrack = 1.00 \times 10^{- 7}\text{~M} + ( + 0.0042\text{~M}) = 0.0042\text{~M}}
\end{array}$$

**C** We can now calculate *K*<sub>a</sub> by inserting the final concentrations into the equilibrium constant expression:

$$K_{\text{a}} = \frac{\lbrack\text{H}^{+}\rbrack\lbrack\text{CH}_{3}\text{CO}_{2}{}^{-}\rbrack}{\lbrack\text{CH}_{3}\text{CO}_{2}\text{H}\rbrack} = \frac{(0.0042)(0.0042)}{1.00} = 1.8 \times 10^{- 5}$$

The p*K*<sub>a</sub> is the negative logarithm of *K*<sub>a</sub>: p*K*<sub>a</sub> = −log *K*<sub>a</sub> = −log(1.8 × 10<sup>−5</sup>) = 4.74.

Exercise

Picric acid is the common name for 2,4,6-trinitrophenol, a derivative of phenol (C<sub>6</sub>H<sub>5</sub>OH) in which three H atoms are replaced by nitro (--NO<sub>2</sub>) groups. The presence of the nitro groups removes electron density from the phenyl ring, making picric acid a much stronger acid than phenol (p*K*<sub>a</sub> = 9.99). The nitro groups also make picric acid potentially explosive, as you might expect based on its chemical similarity to 2,4,6-trinitrotoluene, better known as TNT. A 0.20 M solution of picric acid is 73% ionized at 25°C. Calculate *K*<sub>a</sub> and p*K*<sub>a</sub> for picric acid.

![Chemical illustration from Chapter 16: Aqueous Acid--Base Equilibriums](/images/section_20/c974919c3a52b0b5253123325194e79d.jpg)

**Answer:** *K*<sub>a</sub> = 0.39; p*K*<sub>a</sub> = 0.41

### Example 7

A 1.0 M aqueous solution of ammonia has a pH of 11.63 at 25°C. Calculate *K*<sub>b</sub> and p*K*<sub>b</sub> for ammonia.

**Given:** analytical concentration and pH

**Asked for:** *K*<sub>b</sub> and p*K*<sub>b</sub>

**Strategy:**

**A** Write the balanced equilibrium equation for the reaction and derive the equilibrium constant expression.

**B** Use the data given and the stoichiometry of the reaction to construct a table showing the initial concentrations, the changes in concentrations, and the final concentrations for all species in the equilibrium constant expression.

**C** Substitute the final concentrations into the equilibrium constant expression and calculate the *K*<sub>b</sub>. Take the negative logarithm of *K*<sub>b</sub> to obtain the p*K*<sub>b</sub>.

**Solution:**

**A** The balanced equilibrium equation for the reaction of ammonia with water is as follows:

$$\left. \text{NH}_{3}\text{(aq)} + \text{H}_{2}\text{O(l)}\rightleftharpoons\text{NH}_{4}{}^{+}\text{(aq)} + \text{OH}^{-}\text{(aq)} \right.$$

and the equilibrium constant expression is as follows:

$$K_{\text{b}} = \frac{\lbrack\text{NH}_{4}{}^{+}\rbrack\lbrack\text{OH}^{-}\rbrack}{\lbrack\text{NH}_{3}\rbrack}$$

Remember that water does not appear in the equilibrium constant expression for *K*<sub>b</sub>.

**B** To calculate *K*<sub>b</sub>, we need to know the equilibrium concentrations of NH<sub>3</sub>, NH<sub>4</sub><sup>+</sup>, and OH<sup>−</sup>. The initial concentration of NH<sub>3</sub> is the analytical concentration, 1.0 M, and the initial concentrations of NH<sub>4</sub><sup>+</sup> and OH<sup>−</sup> are 0 M and 1.00 × 10<sup>−7</sup> M, respectively. In this case, we are given the pH of the solution, which allows us to calculate the final concentration of one species (OH<sup>−</sup>) directly, rather than the change in concentration. Recall that p*K*<sub>w</sub> = pH + pOH = 14.00 at 25°C. Thus pOH = 14.00 − pH = 14.00 − 11.63 = 2.37, and \[OH<sup>−</sup>\]<sub>f</sub> = 10<sup>−2.37</sup> = 4.3 × 10<sup>−3</sup> M. Our data thus far are listed in the following table.

````````{list-table}
:header-rows: 2

- - $\left. \text{NH}_{3}\text{(aq)}\rightleftharpoons\text{NH}_{4}{}^{+}\text{(aq)} + \text{OH}^{-}\text{(aq)} \right.$
  - $\left. \text{NH}_{3}\text{(aq)}\rightleftharpoons\text{NH}_{4}{}^{+}\text{(aq)} + \text{OH}^{-}\text{(aq)} \right.$
  - $\left. \text{NH}_{3}\text{(aq)}\rightleftharpoons\text{NH}_{4}{}^{+}\text{(aq)} + \text{OH}^{-}\text{(aq)} \right.$
  - $\left. \text{NH}_{3}\text{(aq)}\rightleftharpoons\text{NH}_{4}{}^{+}\text{(aq)} + \text{OH}^{-}\text{(aq)} \right.$
- -  
  - \[NH<sub>3</sub>\]
  - \[NH<sub>4</sub><sup>+</sup>\]
  - \[OH<sup>−</sup>\]
- - initial
  - 1.0
  - 0
  - 1.00 × 10<sup>−7</sup>
- - change
  -  
  -  
  -  
- - final
  -  
  -  
  - 4.3 × 10<sup>−3</sup>

````````

The final \[OH<sup>−</sup>\] is much greater than the initial \[H<sup>+</sup>\], so the change in \[OH<sup>−</sup>\] is as follows:

Δ\[OH<sup>−</sup>\] = (4.3 × 10<sup>−3</sup> M) − (1.00 × 10<sup>−7</sup> M) ≈ 4.3 × 10<sup>−3</sup> M

The stoichiometry of the reaction tells us that 1 mol of NH<sub>3</sub> is converted to NH<sub>4</sub><sup>+</sup> for each 1 mol of OH<sup>−</sup> formed, so

Δ\[NH<sub>4</sub><sup>+</sup>\] = +4.3 × 10<sup>−3</sup> M and Δ\[NH<sub>3</sub>\] = −4.3 ×10<sup>−3</sup> M

We can now insert these values for the changes in concentrations into the table, which enables us to complete the table.

````````{list-table}
:header-rows: 2

- - $\left. \text{H}_{2}\text{O(l)} + \text{NH}_{3}\text{(aq)}\rightleftharpoons\text{NH}_{4}{}^{+}\text{(aq)} + \text{OH}^{-}\text{(aq)} \right.$
  - $\left. \text{H}_{2}\text{O(l)} + \text{NH}_{3}\text{(aq)}\rightleftharpoons\text{NH}_{4}{}^{+}\text{(aq)} + \text{OH}^{-}\text{(aq)} \right.$
  - $\left. \text{H}_{2}\text{O(l)} + \text{NH}_{3}\text{(aq)}\rightleftharpoons\text{NH}_{4}{}^{+}\text{(aq)} + \text{OH}^{-}\text{(aq)} \right.$
  - $\left. \text{H}_{2}\text{O(l)} + \text{NH}_{3}\text{(aq)}\rightleftharpoons\text{NH}_{4}{}^{+}\text{(aq)} + \text{OH}^{-}\text{(aq)} \right.$
- -  
  - \[NH<sub>3</sub>\]
  - \[NH<sub>4</sub><sup>+</sup>\]
  - \[OH<sup>−</sup>\]
- - initial
  - 1.0
  - 0
  - 1.00 × 10<sup>−7</sup>
- - change
  - −4.3 × 10<sup>−3</sup>
  - +4.3 × 10<sup>−3</sup>
  - +4.3 × 10<sup>−3</sup>
- - final
  - 1.0
  - 4.3 × 10<sup>−3</sup>
  - 4.3 × 10<sup>−3</sup>

````````

**C** Inserting the final concentrations into the equilibrium constant expression gives *K*<sub>b</sub>:

$$K_{\text{b}} = \frac{\lbrack\text{NH}_{4}{}^{+}\rbrack\lbrack\text{OH}^{-}\rbrack}{\lbrack\text{NH}_{3}\rbrack} = \frac{{(4.3 \times 10^{- 3})}^{2}}{1.0} = 1.8 \times 10^{- 5}$$

and p*K*<sub>b</sub> = −log *K*<sub>b</sub> = 4.74.

The *K*<sub>b</sub> and the p*K*<sub>b</sub> for ammonia are almost exactly the same as the *K*<sub>a</sub> and the p*K*<sub>a</sub> for acetic acid at 25°C. In other words, ammonia is almost exactly as strong a base as acetic acid is an acid. Consequently, the extent of the ionization reaction in an aqueous solution of ammonia at a given concentration is the same as in an aqueous solution of acetic acid at the same concentration.

Exercise

The pH of a 0.050 M solution of pyridine (C<sub>6</sub>H<sub>5</sub>N) is 8.96 at 25°C. Calculate *K*<sub>b</sub> and p*K*<sub>b</sub> for pyridine.

![Chemical illustration from Chapter 16: Aqueous Acid--Base Equilibriums](/images/section_20/99ee5e20d083214de220d4507fefefc0.jpg)

 

**Answer:** *K*<sub>b</sub> = 1.7 × 10<sup>−9</sup>; p*K*<sub>b</sub> = 8.77

### Calculating Percent Ionization from *K*<sub>a</sub> or *K*<sub>b</sub>

When carrying out a laboratory analysis, chemists frequently need to know the concentrations of all species in solution. Because the reactivity of a weak acid or a weak base is usually very different from the reactivity of its conjugate base or acid, we often need to know the percent ionization of a solution of an acid or a base to understand a chemical reaction. The percent ionization is defined as follows:

(src-averill-1-0-ch16-s04-s02-eq01)=

Equation 16.41

$$\text{percent~ionization~}ο\text{f~acid} = \frac{\lbrack\text{H}^{+}\rbrack}{C_{\text{HA}}} \times 100$$

(src-averill-1-0-ch16-s04-s02-eq02)=

Equation 16.42

$$\text{percent~ionization~}ο\text{f~base} = \frac{\lbrack\text{OH}^{-}\rbrack}{C_{\text{B}}} \times 100$$

One way to determine the concentrations of species in solutions of weak acids and bases is a variation of the tabular method we used previously to determine *K*<sub>a</sub> and *K*<sub>b</sub> values. As a demonstration, we will calculate the concentrations of all species and the percent ionization in a 0.150 M solution of formic acid at 25°C. The data in [Table 16.2 \"Values of \"](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s02-t01) show that formic acid (*K*<sub>a</sub> = 1.8 × 10<sup>−4</sup> at 25°C) is a slightly stronger acid than acetic acid. The equilibrium equation for the ionization of formic acid in water is as follows:

Equation 16.43

$$\left. \text{HCO}_{2}\text{H(aq)}\rightleftharpoons\text{H}^{+}\text{(aq)} + \text{HCO}_{2}{}^{-}\text{(aq)} \right.$$

and the equilibrium constant expression for this reaction is as follows:

Equation 16.44

$$K_{\text{a}} = \frac{\lbrack\text{H}^{+}\rbrack\lbrack\text{HCO}_{2}{}^{-}\rbrack}{\lbrack\text{HCO}_{2}\text{H}\rbrack}$$

We set the initial concentration of HCO<sub>2</sub>H equal to 0.150 M, and that of HCO<sub>2</sub><sup>−</sup> is 0 M. The initial concentration of H<sup>+</sup> is 1.00 × 10<sup>−7</sup> M due to the autoionization of water. Because the equilibrium constant for the ionization reaction is small, the equilibrium will lie to the left, favoring the unionized form of the acid. Hence we can define *x* as the amount of formic acid that dissociates.

If the change in \[HCO<sub>2</sub>H\] is −*x*, then the change in \[H<sup>+</sup>\] and \[HCO<sub>2</sub><sup>−</sup>\] is +*x*. The final concentration of each species is the sum of its initial concentration and the change in concentration, as summarized in the following table.

````````{list-table}
:header-rows: 2

- - $\left. \text{HCO}_{2}\text{H(aq)}\rightleftharpoons\text{H}^{+}\text{(aq)} + \text{HCO}_{2}{}^{-}\text{(aq)} \right.$
  - $\left. \text{HCO}_{2}\text{H(aq)}\rightleftharpoons\text{H}^{+}\text{(aq)} + \text{HCO}_{2}{}^{-}\text{(aq)} \right.$
  - $\left. \text{HCO}_{2}\text{H(aq)}\rightleftharpoons\text{H}^{+}\text{(aq)} + \text{HCO}_{2}{}^{-}\text{(aq)} \right.$
  - $\left. \text{HCO}_{2}\text{H(aq)}\rightleftharpoons\text{H}^{+}\text{(aq)} + \text{HCO}_{2}{}^{-}\text{(aq)} \right.$
- -  
  - \[HCO<sub>2</sub>H\]
  - \[H<sup>+</sup>\]
  - \[HCO<sub>2</sub><sup>−</sup>\]
- - initial
  - 0.150
  - 1.00 × 10<sup>−7</sup>
  - 0
- - change
  - −*x*
  - \+*x*
  - \+*x*
- - final
  - (0.150 − *x*)
  - (1.00 × 10<sup>−7</sup> + *x*)
  - *x*

````````

We can calculate *x* by substituting the final concentrations from the table into the equilibrium constant expression:

$$K_{\text{a}} = \frac{\lbrack\text{H}^{+}\rbrack\lbrack\text{HCO}_{2}{}^{-}\rbrack}{\lbrack\text{HCO}_{2}\text{H}\rbrack} = \frac{(1.00 \times 10^{- 7} + x)x}{0.150 - x}$$

Because the ionization constant *K*<sub>a</sub> is small, *x* is likely to be small compared with the initial concentration of formic acid: (0.150 − *x*) M ≈ 0.150 M. Moreover, \[H<sup>+</sup>\] due to the autoionization of water (1.00 × 10<sup>−7</sup> M) is likely to be negligible compared with \[H<sup>+</sup>\] due to the dissociation of formic acid: (1.00 × 10<sup>−7</sup> + *x*) M ≈ *x* M. Inserting these values into the equilibrium constant expression and solving for *x*,

$$\begin{array}{rcl}
K_{\text{a}} & {= \frac{x^{2}}{0.150}} & {= 1.8 \times 10^{- 4}} \\
 & x & {= 5.2 \times 10^{- 3}}
\end{array}$$

We can now calculate the concentrations of the species present in a 0.150 M formic acid solution by inserting this value of *x* into the expressions in the last line of the table:

$$\begin{aligned}
{\lbrack\text{HCO}_{2}\text{H}\rbrack} & {= (0.150 - x)\text{~M} = 0.145\text{~M}} \\
{\lbrack\text{HCO}_{2}\rbrack} & {= x = 5.2 \times 10^{- 3}\text{~M}} \\
{\lbrack\text{H}^{+}\rbrack} & {= (1.00 \times 10^{- 7} + x)\text{~M} = 5.2 \times 10^{- 3}\text{~M}}
\end{aligned}$$

Thus the pH of the solution is --log(5.2 × 10<sup>−3</sup>) = 2.28. We can also use these concentrations to calculate the fraction of the original acid that is ionized. In this case, the percent ionization is the ratio of \[H<sup>+</sup>\] (or \[HCO<sub>2</sub><sup>−</sup>\]) to the analytical concentration, multiplied by 100 to give a percentage:

$$\text{percent~ionization} = \frac{\lbrack\text{H}^{+}\rbrack}{C_{\text{HA}}} \times 100 = \frac{5.2 \times 10^{- 3}\text{~M}}{0.150} \times 100 = 3.5\%$$

*Always check to make sure that any simplifying assumption was valid.* As a general rule of thumb, approximations such as those used here are valid *only* if the quantity being neglected is no more than about 5% of the quantity to which it is being added or from which it is being subtracted. If the quantity that was neglected is much greater than about 5%, then the approximation is probably not valid, and you should go back and solve the problem using the quadratic formula. In the previous demonstration, both simplifying assumptions were justified: the percent ionization is only 3.5%, which is well below the approximately 5% limit, and the 1.00 × 10<sup>−7</sup> M \[H<sup>+</sup>\] due to the autoionization of water is much, much less than the 5.2 × 10<sup>−3</sup> M \[H<sup>+</sup>\] due to the ionization of formic acid.

As a general rule, the \[H<sup>+</sup>\] contribution due to the autoionization of water can be ignored as long as the product of the acid or the base ionization constant and the analytical concentration of the acid or the base is at least 10 times greater than the \[H<sup>+</sup>\] or \[OH<sup>−</sup>\] from the autoionization of water---that is, if

(src-averill-1-0-ch16-s04-s02-eq05)=

Equation 16.45

*K*<sub>a</sub>*C*<sub>HA</sub> ≥ 10(1.00 × 10<sup>−7</sup>) = 1.0 × 10<sup>−6</sup>

or

(src-averill-1-0-ch16-s04-s02-eq06)=

Equation 16.46

*K*<sub>b</sub>*C*<sub>B</sub> ≥ 10(1.00 × 10<sup>−7</sup>) = 1.0 × 10<sup>−6</sup>

By substituting the appropriate values for the formic acid solution into [Equation 16.45](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s04-s02-eq05), we see that the simplifying assumption is valid in this case:

Equation 16.47

*K*<sub>a</sub>*C*<sub>HA</sub> = (1.8 × 10<sup>−4</sup>)(0.150) = 2.7 × 10<sup>−5</sup> \> 1.0 × 10<sup>−6</sup>

Doing this simple calculation before solving this type of problem saves time and allows you to write simplified expressions for the final concentrations of the species present. In practice, it is necessary to include the \[H<sup>+</sup>\] contribution due to the autoionization of water only for extremely dilute solutions of very weak acids or bases.

Example 8 illustrates how the procedure outlined previously can be used to calculate the pH of a solution of a weak base.

### Example 8

Calculate the pH and percent ionization of a 0.225 M solution of ethylamine (CH<sub>3</sub>CH<sub>2</sub>NH<sub>2</sub>), which is used in the synthesis of some dyes and medicines. The p*K*<sub>b</sub> of ethylamine is 3.19 at 20°C.

**Given:** concentration and p*K*<sub>b</sub>

**Asked for:** pH and percent ionization

**Strategy:**

**A** Write the balanced equilibrium equation for the reaction and the equilibrium constant expression. Calculate *K*<sub>b</sub> from p*K*<sub>b</sub>.

**B** Use [Equation 16.45](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s04-s02-eq05) to see whether you can ignore \[H<sup>+</sup>\] due to the autoionization of water. Then use a tabular format to write expressions for the final concentrations of all species in solution. Substitute these values into the equilibrium equation and solve for \[OH<sup>−</sup>\]. Use [Equation 16.42](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s04-s02-eq02) to calculate the percent ionization.

**C** Use the relationship *K*<sub>w</sub> = \[OH<sup>−</sup>\]\[H<sup>+</sup>\] to obtain \[H<sup>+</sup>\]. Then calculate the pH of the solution.

**Solution:**

**A** We begin by writing the balanced equilibrium equation for the reaction:

$$\left. \text{CH}_{3}\text{CH}_{2}\text{NH}_{2}\text{(aq)} + \text{H}_{2}\text{O(l)}\rightleftharpoons\text{CH}_{3}\text{CH}_{2}\text{NH}_{3}{}^{+}\text{(aq)} + \text{OH}^{-}\text{(aq)} \right.$$

The corresponding equilibrium constant expression is as follows:

$$K_{\text{b}} = \frac{\lbrack\text{CH}_{3}\text{CH}_{2}\text{NH}_{3}{}^{+}\rbrack\lbrack\text{OH}^{-}\rbrack}{\lbrack\text{CH}_{3}\text{CH}_{2}\text{NH}_{2}\rbrack}$$

From the p*K*<sub>b</sub>, we have *K*<sub>b</sub> = 10<sup>−3.19</sup> = 6.5 × 10<sup>−4</sup>.

**B** To calculate the pH, we need to determine the H<sup>+</sup> concentration. Unfortunately, H<sup>+</sup> does not appear in either the chemical equation or the equilibrium constant expression. However, \[H<sup>+</sup>\] and \[OH<sup>−</sup>\] in an aqueous solution are related by *K*<sub>w</sub> = \[H<sup>+</sup>\]\[OH<sup>−</sup>\]. Hence if we can determine \[OH<sup>−</sup>\], we can calculate \[H<sup>+</sup>\] and then the pH. The initial concentration of CH<sub>3</sub>CH<sub>2</sub>NH<sub>2</sub> is 0.225 M, and the initial \[OH<sup>−</sup>\] is 1.00 × 10<sup>−7</sup> M. Because ethylamine is a weak base, the extent of the reaction will be small, and it makes sense to let *x* equal the amount of CH<sub>3</sub>CH<sub>2</sub>NH<sub>2</sub> that reacts with water. The change in \[CH<sub>3</sub>CH<sub>2</sub>NH<sub>2</sub>\] is therefore −*x*, and the change in both \[CH<sub>3</sub>CH<sub>2</sub>NH<sub>3</sub><sup>+</sup>\] and \[OH<sup>−</sup>\] is +*x*. To see whether the autoionization of water can safely be ignored, we substitute *K*<sub>b</sub> and *C*<sub>B</sub> into [Equation 16.46](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s04-s02-eq06):

*K*<sub>b</sub>*C*<sub>B</sub> = (6.5 × 10<sup>−4</sup>)(0.225) = 1.5 × 10<sup>−4</sup> \> 1.0 × 10<sup>−6</sup>

Thus the simplifying assumption is valid, and we will not include \[OH<sup>−</sup>\] due to the autoionization of water in our calculations.

````````{list-table}
:header-rows: 2

- - $\left. \text{H}_{2}\text{O(1)} + \text{CH}_{3}\text{CH}_{2}\text{NH}_{2}\text{(aq)}\rightleftharpoons\text{CH}_{3}\text{CH}_{2}\text{NH}_{3}{}^{+}\text{(aq)} + \text{OH}^{-}\text{(aq)} \right.$
  - $\left. \text{H}_{2}\text{O(1)} + \text{CH}_{3}\text{CH}_{2}\text{NH}_{2}\text{(aq)}\rightleftharpoons\text{CH}_{3}\text{CH}_{2}\text{NH}_{3}{}^{+}\text{(aq)} + \text{OH}^{-}\text{(aq)} \right.$
  - $\left. \text{H}_{2}\text{O(1)} + \text{CH}_{3}\text{CH}_{2}\text{NH}_{2}\text{(aq)}\rightleftharpoons\text{CH}_{3}\text{CH}_{2}\text{NH}_{3}{}^{+}\text{(aq)} + \text{OH}^{-}\text{(aq)} \right.$
  - $\left. \text{H}_{2}\text{O(1)} + \text{CH}_{3}\text{CH}_{2}\text{NH}_{2}\text{(aq)}\rightleftharpoons\text{CH}_{3}\text{CH}_{2}\text{NH}_{3}{}^{+}\text{(aq)} + \text{OH}^{-}\text{(aq)} \right.$
- -  
  - \[CH<sub>3</sub>CH<sub>2</sub>NH<sub>2</sub>\]
  - \[CH<sub>3</sub>CH<sub>2</sub>NH<sub>3</sub><sup>+</sup>\]
  - \[OH<sup>−</sup>\]
- - initial
  - 0.225
  - 0
  - 1.00 × 10<sup>−7</sup>
- - change
  - −*x*
  - \+*x*
  - \+*x*
- - final
  - (0.225 − *x*)
  - *x*
  - *x*

````````

Substituting the quantities from the last line of the table into the equilibrium constant expression,

$$K_{\text{b}} = \frac{\lbrack\text{CH}_{3}\text{CH}_{2}\text{NH}_{3}{}^{+}\rbrack\lbrack\text{OH}^{-}\rbrack}{\lbrack\text{CH}_{3}\text{CH}_{2}\text{NH}_{2}\rbrack} = \frac{(x)(x)}{0.225 - x} = 6.5 \times 10^{- 4}$$

As before, we assume the amount of CH<sub>3</sub>CH<sub>2</sub>NH<sub>2</sub> that ionizes is small compared with the initial concentration, so \[CH<sub>3</sub>CH<sub>2</sub>NH<sub>2</sub>\]<sub>f</sub> = 0.225 − *x* ≈ 0.225. With this assumption, we can simplify the equilibrium equation and solve for *x*:

$$\begin{array}{rcl}
K_{\text{b}} & {= \frac{x^{2}}{0.225}} & {= 6.5 \times 10^{- 4}} \\
 & x & {= 0.012 = {\lbrack\text{CH}_{3}\text{CH}_{2}\text{NH}_{3}{}^{+}\rbrack}_{\text{f}} = {\lbrack\text{OH}^{-}\rbrack}_{\text{f}}}
\end{array}$$

The percent ionization is therefore

$$\text{percent~ionization} = \frac{\lbrack\text{OH}^{-}\rbrack}{C_{\text{B}}} \times 100 = \frac{0.012\text{~M}}{0.225\text{~M}} \times 100 = 5.4\%$$

which is at the upper limit of the approximately 5% range that can be ignored. The final hydroxide concentration is thus 0.012 M.

**C** We can now determine the \[H<sup>+</sup>\] using the expression for *K*<sub>w</sub>:

$$\begin{aligned}
K_{\text{w}} & {= \lbrack\text{OH}^{-}\rbrack\lbrack\text{H}^{+}\rbrack} \\
{1.01 \times 10^{- 14}} & {= (0.012\text{~M})\lbrack\text{H}^{+}\rbrack} \\
{8.4 \times 10^{- 13}\text{~M}} & {= \lbrack\text{H}^{+}\rbrack}
\end{aligned}$$

The pH of the solution is −log(8.4 × 10<sup>−13</sup>) = 12.08. Alternatively, we could have calculated pOH as −log(0.012) = 1.92 and determined the pH as follows:

$$\begin{aligned}
{\text{pH}\operatorname{~+~}\text{pOH}} & {= \text{p}K_{\text{w}} = 14.00} \\
\text{pH} & {= 14.00 - 1.92 = 12.08}
\end{aligned}$$

The two methods are equivalent.

Exercise

Aromatic amines, in which the nitrogen atom is bonded directly to a phenyl ring (−C<sub>6</sub>H<sub>5</sub>) tend to be much weaker bases than simple alkylamines. For example, aniline (C<sub>6</sub>H<sub>5</sub>NH<sub>2</sub>) has a p*K*<sub>b</sub> of 9.13 at 25°C. What is the pH of a 0.050 M solution of aniline?

**Answer:** 8.78

The previous examples illustrate a key difference between solutions of strong acids and bases and solutions of weak acids and bases. Because strong acids and bases ionize essentially completely in water, the percent ionization is always approximately 100%, regardless of the concentration. In contrast, the percent ionization in solutions of weak acids and bases is small and depends on the analytical concentration of the weak acid or base. As illustrated for benzoic acid in [Figure 16.16 \"The Relationship between the Analytical Concentration of a Weak Acid and Percent Ionization\"](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s04-s02-f01), the percent ionization of a weak acid or a weak base actually *increases* as its analytical concentration decreases. The percent ionization also increases as the magnitude of *K*<sub>a</sub> and *K*<sub>b</sub> increases.

(src-averill-1-0-ch16-s04-s02-f01)=

Figure 16.16 The Relationship between the Analytical Concentration of a Weak Acid and Percent Ionization

![Figure 16.16 The Relationship between the Analytical Concentration of a Weak Acid and Percent Ionization](/images/section_20/c599c91da014c40b138945694bbf7169.jpg)

As shown here for benzoic acid (C<sub>6</sub>H<sub>5</sub>CO<sub>2</sub>H), the percent ionization decreases as the analytical concentration of a weak acid increases.

Unlike the *K*<sub>a</sub> or the *K*<sub>b</sub>, the percent ionization is not a constant for weak acids and bases but depends on *both* the *K*<sub>a</sub> or the *K*<sub>b</sub> *and* the analytical concentration. Consequently, the procedure in Example 8 must be used to calculate the percent ionization and pH for solutions of weak acids and bases. Example 9 and its corresponding exercise demonstrate that the combination of a dilute solution and a relatively large *K*<sub>a</sub> or *K*<sub>b</sub> can give a percent ionization much greater than 5%, making it necessary to use the quadratic equation to determine the concentrations of species in solution.

````````````{important} Note the Pattern

The percent ionization in a solution of a weak acid or a weak base *increases* as the analytical concentration *decreases* and as the *K*<sub>a</sub> or the *K*<sub>b</sub> *increases*.

````````````

### Example 9

Benzoic acid (C<sub>6</sub>H<sub>5</sub>CO<sub>2</sub>H) is used in the food industry as a preservative and medically as an antifungal agent. Its p*K*<sub>a</sub> at 25°C is 4.20, making it a somewhat stronger acid than acetic acid. Calculate the percentage of benzoic acid molecules that are ionized in each solution.

1.  a 0.0500 M solution
2.  a 0.00500 M solution

**Given:** concentrations and p*K*<sub>a</sub>

**Asked for:** percent ionization

**Strategy:**

**A** Write both the balanced equilibrium equation for the ionization reaction and the equilibrium equation ([Equation 16.15](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s02-eq02)). Use [Equation 16.25](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02-s02-eq12) to calculate the *K*<sub>a</sub> from the p*K*<sub>a</sub>.

**B** For both the concentrated solution and the dilute solution, use a tabular format to write expressions for the final concentrations of all species in solution. Substitute these values into the equilibrium equation and solve for \[C<sub>6</sub>H<sub>5</sub>CO<sub>2</sub><sup>−</sup>\]<sub>f</sub> for each solution.

**C** Use the values of \[C<sub>6</sub>H<sub>5</sub>CO<sub>2</sub><sup>−</sup>\]<sub>f</sub> and [Equation 16.41](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s04-s02-eq01) to calculate the percent ionization.

**Solution:**

**A** If we abbreviate benzoic acid as PhCO<sub>2</sub>H where Ph = --C<sub>6</sub>H<sub>5</sub>, the balanced equilibrium equation for the ionization reaction and the equilibrium equation can be written as follows:

$$\begin{array}{r}
\left. \text{PhCO}_{2}\text{H(aq)}\rightleftharpoons\text{H}^{+}\text{(aq)} + \text{PhCO}_{2}{}^{-}\text{(aq)} \right. \\
{K_{\text{a}} = \frac{\lbrack\text{H}^{+}\rbrack\lbrack\text{PhCO}_{2}{}^{-}\rbrack}{\lbrack\text{PhCO}_{2}\text{H}\rbrack}}
\end{array}$$

From the p*K*<sub>a</sub>, we have *K*<sub>a</sub> = 10<sup>−4.20</sup> = 6.3 × 10<sup>−5</sup>.

1.  **B** For the more concentrated solution, we set up our table of initial concentrations, changes in concentrations, and final concentrations:

    ````````{list-table}
    :header-rows: 2

    - - $\left. \text{PhCO}_{2}\text{H(aq)}\rightleftharpoons\text{H}^{+}\text{(aq)} + \text{PhCO}_{2}{}^{-}\text{(aq)} \right.$
      - $\left. \text{PhCO}_{2}\text{H(aq)}\rightleftharpoons\text{H}^{+}\text{(aq)} + \text{PhCO}_{2}{}^{-}\text{(aq)} \right.$
      - $\left. \text{PhCO}_{2}\text{H(aq)}\rightleftharpoons\text{H}^{+}\text{(aq)} + \text{PhCO}_{2}{}^{-}\text{(aq)} \right.$
      - $\left. \text{PhCO}_{2}\text{H(aq)}\rightleftharpoons\text{H}^{+}\text{(aq)} + \text{PhCO}_{2}{}^{-}\text{(aq)} \right.$
    - -  
      - \[PhCO<sub>2</sub>H\]
      - \[H<sup>+</sup>\]
      - \[PhCO<sub>2</sub><sup>−</sup>\]
    - - initial
      - 0.0500
      - 1.00 × 10<sup>−7</sup>
      - 0
    - - change
      - −*x*
      - \+*x*
      - \+*x*
    - - final
      - (0.0500 − *x*)
      - (1.00 × 10<sup>−7</sup> + *x*)
      - *x*

    ````````

    Inserting the expressions for the final concentrations into the equilibrium equation and making our usual assumptions, that \[PhCO<sub>2</sub><sup>−</sup>\] and \[H<sup>+</sup>\] are negligible due to the autoionization of water,

    $$\begin{array}{rclc}
    K_{\text{a}} & {= \frac{\lbrack\text{H}^{+}\rbrack\lbrack\text{PhCO}_{2}{}^{-}\rbrack}{\lbrack\text{PhCO}_{2}\text{H}\rbrack}} & {= \frac{(x)(x)}{0.0500 - x}} & {= \frac{x^{2}}{0.0500} = 6.3 \times 10^{- 5}} \\
     & {1.8 \times 10^{- 3}} & {= x} & 
    \end{array}$$

    This value is less than 5% of 0.0500, so our simplifying assumption is justified, and \[PhCO<sub>2</sub><sup>−</sup>\] at equilibrium is 1.8 × 10<sup>−3</sup> M. We reach the same conclusion using *C*<sub>HA</sub>: *K*<sub>a</sub>*C*<sub>HA</sub> = (6.3 × 10<sup>−5</sup>)(0.0500) = 3.2 × 10<sup>−6</sup> \> 1.0 × 10<sup>−6</sup>.

    **C** The percent ionized is the ratio of the concentration of PhCO<sub>2</sub><sup>−</sup> to the analytical concentration, multiplied by 100:

    $$\text{percent~ionized} = \frac{\lbrack\text{PhCO}_{2}{}^{-}\rbrack}{C_{\text{PhCO}_{2}\text{H}}} \times 100 = \frac{1.8 \times 10^{- 3}}{0.0500} \times 100 = 3.6\%$$

    Because only 3.6% of the benzoic acid molecules are ionized in a 0.0500 M solution, our simplifying assumptions are confirmed.

2.  **B** For the more dilute solution, we proceed in exactly the same manner. Our table of concentrations is therefore as follows:

    ````````{list-table}
    :header-rows: 2

    - - $\left. \text{PhCO}_{2}\text{H(aq)}\rightleftharpoons\text{H}^{+}\text{(aq)} + \text{PhCO}_{2}{}^{-}\text{(aq)} \right.$
      - $\left. \text{PhCO}_{2}\text{H(aq)}\rightleftharpoons\text{H}^{+}\text{(aq)} + \text{PhCO}_{2}{}^{-}\text{(aq)} \right.$
      - $\left. \text{PhCO}_{2}\text{H(aq)}\rightleftharpoons\text{H}^{+}\text{(aq)} + \text{PhCO}_{2}{}^{-}\text{(aq)} \right.$
      - $\left. \text{PhCO}_{2}\text{H(aq)}\rightleftharpoons\text{H}^{+}\text{(aq)} + \text{PhCO}_{2}{}^{-}\text{(aq)} \right.$
    - -  
      - \[PhCO<sub>2</sub>H\]
      - \[H<sup>+</sup>\]
      - \[PhCO<sub>2</sub><sup>−</sup>\]
    - - initial
      - 0.00500
      - 1.00 × 10<sup>−7</sup>
      - 0
    - - change
      - −*x*
      - \+*x*
      - \+*x*
    - - final
      - (0.00500 − *x*)
      - (1.00 × 10<sup>−7</sup> + *x*)
      - *x*

    ````````

    Inserting the expressions for the final concentrations into the equilibrium equation and making our usual simplifying assumptions,

    $$\begin{array}{rclc}
    K_{\text{a}} & {= \frac{\lbrack\text{H}^{+}\rbrack\lbrack\text{PhCO}_{2}{}^{-}\rbrack}{\lbrack\text{PhCO}_{2}\text{H}\rbrack}} & {= \frac{(x)(x)}{0.00500 - x}} & {= \frac{x^{2}}{0.00500} = 6.3 \times 10^{- 5}} \\
     & {5.6 \times 10^{- 4}} & {= x} & 
    \end{array}$$

    Unfortunately, this number is greater than 10% of 0.00500, so our assumption that the fraction of benzoic acid that is ionized in this solution could be neglected and that (0.00500 − *x*) ≈ *x* is not valid. Furthermore, we see that *K*<sub>a</sub>*C*<sub>HA</sub> = (6.3 × 10<sup>−5</sup>)(0.00500) = 3.2 × 10<sup>−7</sup> \< 1.0 × 10<sup>−6</sup>. Thus the relevant equation is as follows:

    $$\frac{x^{2}}{0.00500 - x} = 6.3 \times 10^{- 5}$$

    which must be solved using the quadratic formula. Multiplying out the quantities,

    *x*<sup>2</sup> = (6.3 × 10<sup>−5</sup>)(0.00500 − *x*) = (3.2 × 10<sup>−7</sup>) − (6.3 × 10<sup>−5</sup>)*x*

    Rearranging the equation to fit the standard quadratic equation format,

    *x*<sup>2</sup> + (6.3 × 10<sup>−5</sup>)*x* − (3.2 × 10<sup>−7</sup>) = 0

    This equation can be solved by using the quadratic formula:

    $$\begin{aligned}
    x & {= \frac{- b \pm \sqrt{b^{2} - 4ac}}{2a}} \\
     & {= \frac{- (6.3 \times 10^{- 5}) \pm \sqrt{{(6.3 \times 10^{- 5})}^{2} - 4(1)( - 3.2 \times 10^{- 7})}}{2(1)}} \\
     & {= \frac{- (6.3 \times 10^{- 5}) \pm (1.1 \times 10^{- 3})}{2} = 5.3 \times 10^{- 4}\text{~or} - 5.9 \times 10^{- 4}}
    \end{aligned}$$

    Because a negative *x* value corresponds to a negative \[PhCO<sub>2</sub><sup>−</sup>\], which is not physically meaningful, we use the positive solution: *x* = 5.3 × 10<sup>−4</sup>. Thus \[PhCO<sub>2</sub><sup>−</sup>\] = 5.3 × 10<sup>−4</sup> M.

    **C** The percent ionized is therefore

    $$\text{percent~ionized} = \frac{\lbrack\text{PhCO}_{2}{}^{-}\rbrack}{C_{\text{PhCO}_{2}\text{H}}} \times 100 = \frac{5.3 \times 10^{- 4}}{0.00500} \times 100 = 11\%$$

    In the more dilute solution (*C* = 0.00500 M), 11% of the benzoic acid molecules are ionized versus only 3.6% in the more concentrated solution (*C* = 0.0500 M). Decreasing the analytical concentration by a factor of 10 results in an approximately threefold increase in the percentage of benzoic acid molecules that are ionized.

Exercise

Lactic acid (CH<sub>3</sub>CH(OH)CO<sub>2</sub>H) is a weak acid with a p*K*<sub>a</sub> of 3.86 at 25°C. What percentage of the lactic acid is ionized in each solution?

![Chemical illustration from Chapter 16: Aqueous Acid--Base Equilibriums](/images/section_20/389fd587cf774e1c2c575cdcd6fec516.jpg)

1.  a 0.10 M solution
2.  a 0.0020 M solution

**Answer:**

1.  3.7%
2.  23%

### Determining *K*<sub>eq</sub> from *K*<sub>a</sub> and *K*<sub>b</sub>

In [Section 16.2 \"A Qualitative Description of Acid--Base Equilibriums\"](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s02), you learned how to use *K*<sub>a</sub> and *K*<sub>b</sub> values to qualitatively predict whether reactants or products are favored in an acid--base reaction. Tabulated values of *K*<sub>a</sub> (or p*K*<sub>a</sub>) and *K*<sub>b</sub> (or p*K*<sub>b</sub>), plus the *K*<sub>w</sub>, enable us to quantitatively determine the direction and extent of reaction for a weak acid and a weak base by calculating *K* for the reaction. To illustrate how to do this, we begin by writing the dissociation equilibriums for a weak acid and a weak base and then summing them:

(src-averill-1-0-ch16-s04-s03-eq01)=

Equation 16.48

$$\begin{array}{rll}
\text{acid} & \left. \text{HA}\rightleftharpoons\text{H}^{+} + \text{A}^{-} \right. & K_{\text{a}} \\
\text{base} & \left. \text{B~} + \text{~H}_{2}\text{O}\rightleftharpoons\text{HB}^{+} + \text{OH}^{-} \right. & K_{\text{b}} \\
\text{sum} & \left. \text{HA~} + \text{~B} + \text{~H}_{2}\text{O}\rightleftharpoons\text{H}^{+} + \text{A}^{-} + \text{HB}^{+} + \text{OH}^{-} \right. & {K_{\text{sum}} = K_{\text{a}}K_{\text{b}}}
\end{array}$$

The overall reaction has H<sub>2</sub>O on the left and H<sup>+</sup> and OH<sup>−</sup> on the right, which means it involves the autoionization of water $\left. (\text{H}_{2}\text{O}\rightleftharpoons\text{H}^{+} + \text{OH}^{-}) \right.$ in addition to the acid--base equilibrium in which we are interested. We can obtain an equation that includes only the acid--base equilibrium by simply adding the equation for the reverse of the autoionization of water $\left. (\text{H}^{+} + \text{OH}^{-}\rightleftharpoons\text{H}_{2}\text{O}) \right.$, for which *K* = 1/*K*<sub>w</sub>, to the overall equilibrium in [Equation 16.48](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s04-s03-eq01) and canceling:

(src-averill-1-0-ch16-s04-s03-eq02)=

Equation 16.49

$$\begin{array}{ll}
\left. \text{HA~} + \text{~B} + \ \cancel{\text{H}_{2}\text{O}}\rightleftharpoons\cancel{\text{H}^{+}} + \text{A}^{-} + \text{HB}^{+} + \cancel{\text{OH}^{-}} \right. & {K_{\text{sum}} = K_{\text{a}}K_{\text{b}}} \\
\left. \cancel{\text{H}}^{+} + \cancel{\text{OH}^{-}}\rightleftharpoons\cancel{\text{H}_{2}\text{O}} \right. & {1\text{/}K_{\text{w}}} \\
\left. \text{HA~} + \text{~B}\rightleftharpoons\text{A}^{-} + \text{HB}^{+} \right. & {K = (K_{\text{a}}K_{\text{b}})\text{/}K_{\text{w}}}
\end{array}$$

Thus the equilibrium constant for the reaction of a weak acid with a weak base is the product of the ionization constants of the acid and the base divided by *K*<sub>w</sub>. Example 10 illustrates how to calculate the equilibrium constant for the reaction of a weak acid with a weak base.

### Example 10

Fish tend to spoil rapidly, even when refrigerated. The cause of the resulting "fishy" odor is a mixture of amines, particularly methylamine (CH<sub>3</sub>NH<sub>2</sub>), a volatile weak base (p*K*<sub>b</sub> = 3.34). Fish is often served with a wedge of lemon because lemon juice contains citric acid, a triprotic acid with p*K*<sub>a</sub> values of 3.13, 4.76, and 6.40 that can neutralize amines. Calculate the equilibrium constant for the reaction of excess citric acid with methylamine, assuming that only the first dissociation constant of citric acid is important.

![Chemical illustration from Chapter 16: Aqueous Acid--Base Equilibriums](/images/section_20/90173984c92ca77c4e66e15ee997df00.jpg)

**Given:** p*K*<sub>b</sub> for base and p*K*<sub>a</sub> for acid

**Asked for:** *K*

**Strategy:**

**A** Write the balanced equilibrium equation and the equilibrium constant expression for the reaction.

**B** Convert p*K*<sub>a</sub> and p*K*<sub>b</sub> to *K*<sub>a</sub> and *K*<sub>b</sub> and then use [Equation 16.49](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s04-s03-eq02) to calculate *K*.

**Solution:**

**A** If we abbreviate citric acid as H<sub>3</sub>citrate, the equilibrium equation for its reaction with methylamine is as follows:

$$\left. \text{CH}_{3}\text{NH}_{2}\text{(aq)} + \text{H}_{3}\text{citrate(aq)}\rightleftharpoons\text{CH}_{3}\text{NH}_{3}{}^{+}\text{(aq)} + \text{H}_{2}\text{citrate}^{-}\text{(aq)} \right.$$

The equilibrium constant expression for this reaction is as follows:

$$K = \frac{\lbrack\text{CH}_{3}\text{NH}_{3}{}^{+}\rbrack\lbrack\text{H}_{2}\text{citrate}^{-}\rbrack}{\lbrack\text{CH}_{3}\text{NH}_{2}\rbrack\lbrack\text{H}_{3}\text{citrate}\rbrack}$$

**B** [Equation 16.49](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s04-s03-eq02) is *K* = (*K*<sub>a</sub>*K*<sub>b</sub>)/*K*<sub>w</sub>. Converting p*K*<sub>a</sub> and p*K*<sub>b</sub> to *K*<sub>a</sub> and *K*<sub>b</sub> gives *K*<sub>a</sub> = 10<sup>−3.13</sup> = 7.4 × 10<sup>−4</sup> for citric acid and *K*<sub>b</sub> = 10<sup>−3.34</sup> = 4.6 × 10<sup>−4</sup> for methylamine. Substituting these values into the equilibrium equation,

$$K = \frac{K_{\text{a}}K_{\text{b}}}{K_{\text{w}}} = \frac{(7.4 \times 10^{- 4})(4.6 \times 10^{- 4})}{1.01 \times 10^{- 14}} = 3.4 \times 10^{7}$$

The value of p*K* can also be calculated directly by taking the negative logarithm of both sides of [Equation 16.49](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s04-s03-eq02), which gives

p*K* = p*K*<sub>a</sub> + p*K*<sub>b</sub> − p*K*<sub>w</sub> = 3.13 + 3.34 − 14.00 = −7.53

Thus *K* = 10<sup>−(−7.53)</sup> = 3.4 × 10<sup>7</sup>, in agreement with the earlier value. In either case, the *K* values show that the reaction of citric acid with the volatile, foul-smelling methylamine lies very far to the right, favoring the formation of a much less volatile salt with no odor. This is one reason a little lemon juice helps make less-than-fresh fish more appetizing.

Exercise

Dilute aqueous ammonia solution, often used as a cleaning agent, is also effective as a deodorizing agent. To see why, calculate the equilibrium constant for the reaction of aqueous ammonia with butyric acid (CH<sub>3</sub>CH<sub>2</sub>CH<sub>2</sub>CO<sub>2</sub>H), a particularly foul-smelling substance associated with the odor of rancid butter and smelly socks. The p*K*<sub>b</sub> of ammonia is 4.75, and the p*K*<sub>a</sub> of butyric acid is 4.83.

**Answer:** 2.6 × 10<sup>4</sup>

````````````{important} Summary

If the concentration of one or more of the species in a solution of an acid or a base is determined experimentally, *K*<sub>a</sub> and *K*<sub>b</sub> can be calculated, and *K*<sub>a</sub>, p*K*<sub>a</sub>, *K*<sub>b</sub>, and p*K*<sub>b</sub> can be used to quantitatively describe the composition of solutions of acids and bases. The concentrations of all species present in solution can be determined, as can the pH of the solution and the percentage of the acid or base that is ionized. The equilibrium constant for the reaction of a weak acid with a weak base can be calculated from *K*<sub>a</sub> (or p*K*<sub>a</sub>), *K*<sub>b</sub> (or p*K*<sub>b</sub>), and *K*<sub>w</sub>.

````````````

````````````{important} Key Takeaway

- For a solution of a weak acid or a weak base, the percent ionization increases as the *K*<sub>a</sub> or the *K*<sub>b</sub> increases and as the analytical concentration decreases.

````````````

````````````{important} Key Equations

**Percent ionization of acid**

[Equation 16.41](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s04-s02-eq01): $\frac{\lbrack\text{H}^{+}\rbrack}{C_{\text{HA}}} \times 100$

**Percent ionization of base**

[Equation 16.42](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s04-s02-eq02): $\frac{\lbrack\text{OH}^{-}\rbrack}{C_{\text{B}}} \times 100$

**Equilibrium constant for reaction of a weak acid with a weak base**

[Equation 16.49](/chapters/ch-16-aqueous-acid-base-equilibriums.md#src-averill-1-0-ch16-s04-s03-eq02): $K = \frac{K_{\text{a}}K_{\text{b}}}{K_{\text{w}}}$

````````````

### Conceptual Problems

1.  Explain why the analytical concentration (*C*) of H<sub>2</sub>SO<sub>4</sub> is equal to \[H<sub>2</sub>SO<sub>4</sub>\] + \[HSO<sub>4</sub><sup>−</sup>\] + \[SO<sub>4</sub><sup>2−</sup>\].

2.  Write an expression for the analytical concentration (*C*) of H<sub>3</sub>PO<sub>4</sub> in terms of the concentrations of the species actually present in solution.

3.  For relatively dilute solutions of a weak acid such as acetic acid (CH<sub>3</sub>CO<sub>2</sub>H), the concentration of undissociated acetic acid in solution is often assumed to be the same as the analytical concentration. Explain why this is a valid practice.

4.  How does dilution affect the percent ionization of a weak acid or a weak base?

5.  What is the relationship between the *K*<sub>a</sub> of a weak acid and its percent ionization? Does a compound with a large p*K*<sub>a</sub> value have a higher or a lower percent ionization than a compound with a small p*K*<sub>a</sub> value (assuming the same analytical concentration in both cases)? Explain.

6.  For a dilute solution of a weak acid (HA), show that the pH of the solution can be approximated using the following equation (where *C*<sub>HA</sub> is the analytical concentration of the weak acid):

    $$\text{pH} = - \log\sqrt{K_{\text{a}} \cdot C_{\text{HA}}}$$

    Under what conditions is this approximation valid?

Calculate the pH of a solution prepared by adding 0.100 mol of sodium amide to 1.00 L of water. Does the pH differ appreciably from the pH of a NaOH solution of the same concentration? Why or why not?

What is the equilibrium constant expression for the reaction of phenol with water? Use the information in [Chapter 27 \"Appendix C: Dissociation Constants and p\"](/appendices/appendix-c-dissociation-consta.md#src-averill-1-0-ch96appc) to calculate the pH of the phenol solution.

Write an equation showing the equilibrium reaction and then calculate the p*K*<sub>b</sub> of diethylamine. What is the p*K*<sub>a</sub> of its conjugate acid, the diethylammonium ion? 6.  The p*K*<sub>a</sub> of 3-chlorobutanoic acid (CH<sub>3</sub>CHClCH<sub>2</sub>CO<sub>2</sub>H) is 4.05. What percentage is dissociated in a 1.0 M solution? Do you expect the p*K*<sub>a</sub> of butanoic acid to be greater than or less than the p*K*<sub>a</sub> of 3-chlorobutanoic acid? Why?

8.  The p*K*<sub>a</sub> of Cl<sub>3</sub>CCO<sub>2</sub>H is 0.64. What is the pH of a 0.580 M solution? What percentage of the Cl<sub>3</sub>CCO<sub>2</sub>H is dissociated?

Do you expect the p*K*<sub>b</sub> of (CH<sub>3</sub>)<sub>2</sub>CHNH<sub>2</sub> to be greater than or less than the p*K*<sub>b</sub> of C<sub>6</sub>H<sub>5</sub>NH<sub>2</sub>? Why?
